is also a fractional matching of size
\left|{M}\right|
. The following standard fact (see, e.g.
[
LP09
]
) provides the other direction as well.

Fact 3.1
.

For any graph
G=(V,E)
, and any fractional matching
x\in\mathbb{R}^{E}
, we have
\mu(G)\geqslant 2/3\cdot\left|{x}\right|
. Moreover, if
G
is bipartite, then
\mu(G)\geqslant\left|{x}\right|
.

3.1
Concentration Inequalities

We use the following standard form of Chernoff bound and its extension to negatively correlated variables in
[
PS97
]
.

Proposition 3.2

(Chernoff Bound; cf.
[
DP09
]
)
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be
n
independent random variables in
[0,1]
and
X:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
. For any
\delta>0
and
\mu_{min}\leqslant\Exp\left[X\right]\leqslant\mu_{max}
,

\displaystyle\Prob\left(X\geqslant(1+\delta)\cdot\mu_{max}\right)\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}\cdot\mu_{max}}{2+\delta}\right)\quad,\quad\Prob\left(X\leqslant(1-\delta)\cdot\mu_{min}\right)\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}\cdot\mu_{min}}{2}\right).

Moreover, the upper tail bound continues to hold as long
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are negatively correlated, i.e., for every
S\subseteq[n]
,

\Exp\left[\prod_{i\in S}X_{i}\right]\leqslant\prod_{i\in S}\Exp\left[X_{i}\right].

We also need the extension of Chernoff-Hoeffding bounds to limited independence random variables. We define limited independence hash functions first.

Definition 3.3

(Limited Independence Hash Functions)
.

For integers
r,t,k\geqslant 1
, a family
\mathcal{H}
of hash functions from
[r]
to
[t]
is called a
k
-wise independent hash function iff for any two
k
-subsets
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{k}\subseteq[r]
and
b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{k}\subseteq[t]
,

\underset{h\sim\mathcal{H}}{\Prob}(h(a_{1})=b_{1},h(a_{2})=b_{2},\ldots,h(a_{k})=b_{k})=\frac{1}{t^{k}}.

k
-wise independent hash functions behave like random functions, as long as sets of size at most
k
are considered.
We know that we can store and access these functions in limited space.

Proposition 3.4

(​​
[
MR95
]
)
.

For any integers
r,t,k\geqslant 1
, there is a
k
-wise independent hash function family
\mathcal{H}=\left\{h:[r]\rightarrow[t]\right\}
such that sampling and storing a function
h\in\mathcal{H}
takes
O(k\cdot(\log(r\cdot t)))
space.

We can now state the extension of Chernoff bounds for limited independence hash functions.

Proposition 3.5

(​​
[
SSS95
]
)
.

Let
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}
be
k
-wise independent random variables in
[0,1]
and
X:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
. Then, for any
\delta\geqslant 1
and
\Exp\left[X\right]\leqslant\mu_{max}
, we have,

\displaystyle\Prob(X\geqslant(1+\delta)\cdot\mu_{max})\leqslant\exp\left(-\min\left\{\frac{k}{2},\frac{\delta}{3}\cdot\mu_{max}\right\}\right)

.
We also have the following lower tail bound for any
\delta^{\prime}\leqslant 1
and
\mu_{min}\leqslant\Exp\left[X\right]
.

\displaystyle\Prob(X\leqslant(1-\delta^{\prime})\cdot\mu_{min})\leqslant\exp\left(-\min\left\{\frac{k}{2},\frac{(\delta^{\prime})^{2}}{3}\cdot\mu_{min}\right\}\right).

3.2
Sketching and Streaming Toolkit

Sparse-Recovery Algorithms

We use standard sparse-recovery algorithms combined with a simple randomized test to ensure it can also detect non-sparse inputs; see, e.g.
[
AKM23
, Propositions 3.6 and 3.7]
that construct this using Vandermonde matrices and an equality test
(see also
[
AAD+23
, Proposition A.16]
that explicitly shows how to use a PRGs for degree-2 polynomials in
[
Lov09
,
BV10
]
to ensure that the latter algorithm does not need to store many random bits either).

Proposition 3.6

(Sparse Recovery; cf.
[
DV13
,
AKM23
]
)
.

There exists a single-pass deterministic algorithm that given integers
q,n,m\geqslant 1
and a dynamic stream defining a vector
\phi\in[m]^{n}
,
uses
O(q\cdot\log{(m\cdot n)})
bits of space and recovers
\phi
as long as vector
\phi
is
q
-sparse (meaning
\|\phi\|_{0}\leqslant q
).

Moreover, there is a single-pass randomized algorithm that given
\delta>0
can test if the vector
\phi\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
is
q
-sparse with probability at least
1-\delta

using
O(q\cdot\log{(m\cdot n)}+\log{(n/\delta)})
bits of space.

We note that the use of sparse-recovery algorithms in graph sketching already dates back to the seminal work of
[
AGM12
]
. Moreover, many dynamic streaming algorithms
use a particular application of sparse-recovery in form of
\ell_{0}
-samplers

[
JST11
]
that allows for sampling a single element of a given vector
\phi
in a dynamic stream
in
\mbox{\rm poly}\!\log{(n)}
space (instead of recovering the entire vector only if it is sparse). However, for our applications, working with sparse-recovery algorithms directly is more convenient and thus
we opted to skip using
\ell_{0}
-samplers altogether.

Prior Sketching and Streaming Tools for Matchings

We use a vertex sampling approach due to
[
AKLY16
]
that allows for reducing the number of vertices in a graph in an
oblivious
manner, while preserving its largest matching approximately (see also
[
CCE+16
]
for a related but slightly different result). This is a key subroutine used for finding matchings in dynamic streams in a single pass also. We use the following formulation from
[
AKL16
, Lemma 3.8]
that presents this result explicitly.

Proposition 3.7

(​​
[
AKL16
, Lemma 3.8]
)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be any graph with maximum matching size
\mu(G)=\omega(\log{n})
. For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, suppose we partition the vertices of
G
randomly into
t\geqslant 8\mu(G)/\varepsilon
groups
U_{1},\ldots,U_{t}

by sending each vertex to one group chosen independently and uniformly at random. Let
E_{U}\subseteq E
be a subset of edges such that for any pair of groups
U_{i},U_{j}
for
i\neq j\in[t]
, we pick one arbitrary edge

(x,y)
with
x\in U_{i}
and
y\in U_{j}
(if at least one such edge exists). Then, with high probability,
\mu(E_{U})\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\mu(G)
.

We also use the standard framework of boosting
O(1)
-approximation algorithms for maximum matching to
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithms in dynamic streams for any
\varepsilon>0
.
The original version of this framework is due to
[
McG05
]
which was de-randomized in
[
Tir18
]
and extended to weighted graphs in
[
GKMS19
]
; for bipartite graphs, more efficient reductions are shown in
[
AG11
,
ALT21
]
.

Proposition 3.8

(​​
[
McG05
,
AG11
,
GKMS19
,
ALT21
]
)
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and any integer
n\geqslant 1
, suppose we have an
O(1)
-approximation algorithm for finding a maximum matching on
n
-vertex
unweighted
graphs in dynamic streams using
s(n)
-space and
p(n)
-passes with high probability.
Then, there is a dynamic streaming algorithm with
O(f(\varepsilon)\cdot s(n))
-space and
O(f(\varepsilon)\cdot p(n))
-passes that with high probability finds a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to maximum matching even in
weighted
graphs, for some function
f(\varepsilon)
that depends only on
\varepsilon
.

Furthermore, for general weighted graphs, we can set
f(\varepsilon)=(1/\varepsilon)^{\Theta(1/\varepsilon^{2})}
, for general unweighted graphs, set
f(\varepsilon)=(1/\varepsilon)^{\Theta(1/\varepsilon)}
, and for (un)weighted bipartite graphs, set
f(\varepsilon)=\mbox{\rm poly}(1/\varepsilon)
.

3.3
Two-Party Communication Complexity

We work in the standard two party communication model; we provide some basic definitions here and refer the interested reader to the excellent textbooks
[
KN97
,
RY20
]
for more details.

There are two players Alice and Bob who receive input from
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
respectively. The players jointly compute some function
f
with domain
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
. The players follows some protocol
\pi
to compute
f
.
They have access to a shared tape of randomness, referred to as
public randomness
, in addition to their own
private randomness
.

Alice first sends a message to Bob, followed by a message from Bob to Alice and so on. The last player who receives a message has to output the answer. The total number of rounds is the total number of messages passed between Alice and Bob.
Moreover, the message sent by any player only depends on their private input, the communicated messages so far, the public randomness, and the private randomness.

Definition 3.9
.

For any protocol
\pi
, the
communication cost
of
\pi
, denoted by
\textsc{CC}(\pi)
, is defined as the worst-case (maximum) total length of messages, measured in bits, communicated by the players
on any input. We assume that all
transcripts
, i.e., the set of all messages sent by any player, in
\pi
have the same worst-case length (by padding).

The following standard result relates communication protocols and streaming algorithms.

Proposition 3.10

(cf.
[
AMS96
]
)
.

For any
p\geqslant 1,s\geqslant 1
, and
\delta\in(0,1)
, suppose there is a
p
-pass
s
-space streaming algorithm
A
for some problem
\mathbb{P}
that succeeds with probability at least
\delta
. Then, there also exists a two-party protocol
\pi
with
2p-1
rounds, communication cost
\textsc{CC}(\pi)=O(p\cdot s)
, and success probability at least
\delta
for the same problem
\mathbb{P}
.

Proof.

Consider the stream
\sigma=\sigma_{A}\circ\sigma_{B}
where
\sigma_{A}
(resp.
\sigma_{B}
) is the input to Alice (resp. Bob) in
\pi
(ordered arbitrarily in the stream).
Alice runs
A
on
\sigma_{A}
and sends the memory content to Bob, which allows Bob to continue running
A
on
\sigma_{B}
, and send
the memory content back to Alice to continue running the next pass. This allows the players to run one pass of
A
using communication cost at most
O(s)
and
2
rounds of communication.
The players can continue this, faithfully simulating the
p
passes of the algorithm, and at the end of the last pass, Bob can output the answer of
A
, instead of replying back to Alice. This
requires
2p-1
rounds of communication and
O(p\cdot s)
communication, and has the same success probability as the algorithm
A
.

Proposition

3.10
allows us to translate communication lower bounds into streaming ones.

3.4
Information Cost and Message Compression

We also work with the notion of
information cost
of protocols that originated in
[
CSWY01
]
and has since
found numerous applications (see, e.g.,
[
Wei15
]
for an excellent survey of this topic). There are various definitions of information cost that have been considered depending on the application.
The following definition due to
[
BBCR10
]
is best suited for our purpose.

Definition 3.11
.

For any two-party protocol
\pi
whose inputs are distributed according to some distribution
\mu
, the
(internal) information cost
is defined as:

\textsc{IC}(\pi,\mu):=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{X}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid{\mathsf{R}},{\mathsf{Y}})+\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{Y}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid{\mathsf{R}},{\mathsf{X}}),

where
{\mathsf{X}},{\mathsf{Y}}
denote the random variable for the inputs
X,Y
sampled from
\mu
,
{\mathsf{\Pi}}
denotes the random variable corresponding to the communicated messages, and
{\mathsf{R}}
is the public randomness.

Since a single bit of communication can only carry one bit of information, we can upper bound information cost by the communication cost.

Proposition 3.12

(cf.
[
BBCR10
]
)
.

For any protocol
\pi
on any distribution
\mu
,

\textsc{IC}(\pi,\mu)\leqslant\textsc{CC}(\pi).

We also use
message compression
to reduce communication cost of limited-round protocols close to their information cost.
The following is a simplified version of
[
JPY16
, Theorem 3.4]
.

Proposition 3.13

(​
[
JPY16
, Theorem 3.4]
)
.

For
r\geqslant 1
, any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and input distribution
\mu
, any
r
-round protocol
\pi
can be simulated with error at most
\varepsilon
in
r
-rounds by a protocol
\pi^{\prime}
with communication at most

c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot\left(r/\varepsilon\cdot\textsc{IC}(\pi,\mu)+r^{2}/\varepsilon\right)

for some absolute constant
c_{\textsc{jpy}}\geqslant 1
. The simulation by
\pi^{\prime}
uses a source of public randomness that is independent from any randomness used in
\pi
.

4
The Upper Bound

We present our algorithms in this section.
We start with a randomized greedy algorithm for finding an approximate fractional matching by growing a random maximal independent set (MIS). This algorithm is the power horse of our approach, and, given its general nature, we provide it in an entirely model-independent manner.
We then show how this algorithm—in a non black-box way—can be implemented in dynamic graph streams as an
O(\log\log{n})
-pass semi-streaming algorithm.
Finally, we show how to improve this algorithm to yield an approximation factor of
1+\varepsilon
and prove the following theorem, which formalizes
Result

1
.

Theorem 1
.

There is a randomized algorithm that, given any
n
-vertex graph
G=(V,E)
presented in a dynamic stream, finds an
O(1)
-approximate maximum matching in
G
using
O(n\cdot\mbox{\rm polylog}{(n)})
space
and
O(\log\log{n})
passes with high probability.

Moreover, for any
\varepsilon>0
, the algorithm can be improved to finding a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation even in weighted graphs by increasing the space and number of passes with some
f(\varepsilon)
factor,
for some function
f
that only depends on
\varepsilon
(and takes different forms depending on whether or not the graph is bipartite or weighted).

The rest of this section is dedicated to the proof of this theorem.

4.1
A Random-Order Greedy Algorithm for Matching

We present a model-independent algorithm for computing an approximate fractional matching.
The algorithm follows the strategy of
[
Vel24
]
by computing a random order greedy MIS and letting its complement be a vertex cover. In addition to this, whenever the algorithm inserts a new vertex into the vertex cover, it also puts a certain fractional mass distributed uniformly on some
subset
of the not-yet-covered edges incident to this vertex. At the end, the algorithm further “trims down” these fractional values to turn them into a fractional matching, by reducing the mass on every vertex to become at most one explicitly. Formally, the algorithm is as follows.

Algorithm 1
.

An input graph
G=(V,E)
and an approximation parameter
13
13

13

We note that
\beta
is not exactly the approximation ratio that this algorithm achieves, but rather a quantity that governs this ratio.

\beta\in(0,1/8)
.

1.

Let
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}=\emptyset
and
x_{e}=0
for all
e\in E
.

2.

While
G
is not an empty graph:

(a)

Pick a vertex
u
uniformly at random from
G
.

(b)

Add all neighbors
v\in N(u)
to
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
.

(c)

For any
v\in N(u)
and
e=(v,w)
with
\deg(w)\leqslant\deg(v)
set
x_{e}=\beta/\deg(v)
.

(d)

Remove
u\cup N(u)
and all their edges from
G
.

3.

Create
y\in\mathbb{R}^{E}
initialized with
y=x
. Then, for any vertex
v\in V
with
y_{v}:=\sum_{e\ni v}y_{e}>1
, reduce
y_{v}
to
1
by arbitrarily reducing the fractional values on incident edges of
v
.

4.

Return
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
as a vertex cover,
x
as an intermediate solution, and
y
as a fractional matching.

The following theorem captures the main properties of this algorithm that are important for our purposes. Given how we use this result in the subsequent sections, we provide separate guarantees for the assignments
x
and
y
.

Theorem 2
.

Given any graph
G=(V,E)
and parameter
\beta<1/8
,
Algorithm

1
outputs an integral vertex cover
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
, an intermediate solution
x\in\mathbb{R}^{E}
, and a fractional matching
y\in\mathbb{R}^{E}
such that

\displaystyle\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in E}x_{e}\right]\geqslant\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\Exp\left|{V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}}\right|,

(6)

\displaystyle\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in E}y_{e}\right]\geqslant\left(\frac{1-8\beta}{1-2\beta}\right)\cdot\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in E}x_{e}\right].

(7)

Notice that since
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
is always a vertex cover, the inequality
\left|{V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}}\right|\geqslant\mu(G)
always holds, and, thus, combining
Equation

6
and
Equation

7
in
Theorem

2
also directly implies that

\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in E}y_{e}\right]\geqslant\left(\frac{1-8\beta}{1-2\beta}\right)\cdot\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\mu(G).

(8)

In other words, the fractional matching
y
returned by the algorithm is a multiplicative approximation in terms of
\beta
. However,
we need the separate guarantees of
Theorem

2
for the analysis of our dynamic streaming algorithm.

We now start the proof of
Theorem

2
.
Let
T
denote the number of iterations of the while-loop in
Algorithm

1
(which is a random variable). For each iteration
t\in[T]
of the while-loop, we define:

\displaystyle\begin{split}G^{(t)}&:\text{the graph $G$ in this iteration};\\
n^{(t)}&:\text{the number of vertices in $G^{(t)}$};\\
m^{(t)}&:\text{the number of edges in $G^{(t)}$};\\
u^{(t)}&:\text{the vertex $u$ chosen from $G$ in this iteration in Line~\eqref{line:choose}};\\
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}^{(t)}&:\text{vertices added to $V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}$ in this iteration;}\\
N^{(t)}(v)&:\text{for each $v\in G^{(t)}$, the neighbors of $v$ in $G^{(t)}$};\\
\deg^{(t)}(v)&:\text{for each $v\in G^{(t)}$, the degree of $v$ in $G^{(t)}$};\\
x^{(t)}_{e}&:\text{for each $e\in G^{(t)}$, the value {added} to $x_{e}$ in this iteration};\\
R^{<t}&:\text{the entire random choices made in iterations $1,\ldots,t-1$ by the algorithm}.\end{split}

(9)

The first lemma relates the expected size of the vertex cover computed by
Algorithm

1
and
the intermediate solution
x
that it computes, thus proving
Equation

6
in
Theorem

2
.

Lemma 4.1
.

For the intermediate solution
x\in\mathbb{R}^{E}
computed in
Algorithm

1
, we have

\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in E}x_{e}\right]\geqslant\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\Exp\left|{V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}}\right|.

Proof.

Fix any iteration
t\geqslant 1
in the algorithm and condition on all randomness
R^{<t}
up until this iteration. This fixes the graph
G^{(t)}
.
Over the randomness of iteration
t
, we have,

\Exp\left[\left|{V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}^{(t)}}\right|\mid R^{<t}\right]=\sum_{v\in G^{(t)}}\Prob\left(v\in V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}^{(t)}\mid R^{<t}\right)=\sum_{v\in G^{(t)}}\frac{\deg^{(t)}(v)}{n^{(t)}}=\frac{2m^{(t)}}{n^{(t)}},

where the first equality is by the linearity of expectation, the second is because
v
joins
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}^{(t)}
if
u^{(t)}
is chosen from
N^{(t)}(v)
, and the last is by the handshaking lemma.

On the other hand, we have,

\displaystyle\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in G}x^{(t)}_{e}\mid R^{<t}\right]

\displaystyle=\hskip-4.0pt\sum_{u\in G^{(t)}}\hskip-4.0pt\left(\Prob\left(u^{(t)}=u\mid R^{<t}\right)\cdot\hskip-4.0pt\sum_{v\in N^{(t)}(u)}\hskip-4.0pt\left|{\left\{w\in N^{(t)}(v):\deg^{(t)}(w)\leqslant\deg^{(t)}(v)\right\}}\right|\cdot\frac{\beta}{\deg^{(t)}(v)}\right)

(by the definition of the algorithm)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{(t)}}\sum_{v\in G^{(t)}}\sum_{u\in N^{(t)}(v)}\left|{\left\{w\in N^{(t)}(v):\deg^{(t)}(w)\leqslant\deg^{(t)}(v)\right\}}\right|\cdot\frac{\beta}{\deg^{(t)}(v)}

(as
u^{(t)}
is chosen uniformly from
G^{(t)}
and by re-ordering the sum for each edge)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{(t)}}\sum_{v\in G^{(t)}}\sum_{w\in N^{(t)}(v)}\mathbb{I}\left[\deg^{(t)}(w)\leqslant\deg^{(t)}(v)\right]\cdot\beta

(as
\deg^{(t)}(v)=\left|{N^{(t)}(v)}\right|
by definition and
\mathbb{I}\left[\cdot\right]
is the indicator function)

\displaystyle\geqslant\beta\cdot\frac{m^{(t)}}{n^{(t)}},

since for each
(v,w)
at least one of
\mathbb{I}\left[\deg^{(t)}(w)\leqslant\deg^{(t)}(v)\right]
or
\mathbb{I}\left[\deg^{(t)}(v)\leqslant\deg^{(t)}(w)\right]
is true.

Combining the above two equations implies that, for any choice of
R^{<t}
,

\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in G}x^{(t)}_{e}\mid R^{<t}\right]\geqslant\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\Exp\left[\left|{V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}^{(t)}}\right|\mid R^{<t}\right],

which, by the law of total expectation, implies that

\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in G}x^{(t)}_{e}\right]\geqslant\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\Exp{\left|{V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}^{(t)}}\right|}.

Noting that

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in G}x_{e}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geqslant 1}\sum_{e\in G}x^{(t)}_{e}\qquad\text{and }\qquad\left|{V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}}\right|=\sum_{t\geqslant 1}\left|{V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}^{(t)}}\right|,

and linearity of expectation concludes the proof.

While the size of
x
by
Lemma

4.1
is sufficiently large, unfortunately,
x

is not necessarily a fractional matching. As such, we do need to run the update in Line (
3
) to “trim down”
x
into
y
, which becomes a fractional matching.
The main step of the proof is to show that this step is not going to reduce the size of
x
dramatically, proving
Equation

7
in
Theorem

2
.

Lemma 4.2
.

For the intermediate solution
x\in\mathbb{R}^{E}
and fractional matching
y\in\mathbb{R}^{E}
in
Algorithm

1
,

\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in E}y_{e}\right]\geqslant\left(\frac{1-8\beta}{1-2\beta}\right)\cdot\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in E}x_{e}\right].

To continue, we need a couple of more notation. For any iteration
t\geqslant 1
, define:

\displaystyle x^{(t)}_{v}

\displaystyle:\text{for each $v\in G^{(t)}$, $x^{(t)}_{v}:=\sum_{e\ni v}x^{(t)}_{e}$};

\displaystyle out^{(t)}_{v}

\displaystyle:\text{for each $v\in N^{(t)}(u^{(t)})$, the value {added} to $x^{(t)}_{v}$ by $v$ itself};

\displaystyle in^{(t)}_{v}

\displaystyle:\text{for each $v\in G^{(t)}$, the value {added} to $x^{(t)}_{v}$ by any vertex other than $v$}.

This way, for every
v\in V
and
t\geqslant 1
, we have,

x^{(t)}_{v}=out^{(t)}_{v}+in^{(t)}_{v}.

Notice that
out^{(t)}_{v}
is non-zero in at most one iteration, wherein
v
joins
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
. For that iteration
t
,

x^{(t)}_{v}=\left|{\left\{w\in N^{(t)}(v):\deg^{(t)}(w)\leqslant\deg^{(t)}(v)\right\}}\right|\cdot\frac{\beta}{\deg^{(t)}(v)}\leqslant\beta.

This implies that

\displaystyle x_{v}:=\sum_{t\geqslant 1}x^{(t)}_{v}\leqslant\beta+\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{v}.

(10)

Thus, we only need to focus on bounding
in^{(t)}_{v}
across the iterations. We do this in the following claims. Note that in
Algorithm

1
,
the vertices
w
in Line (
2c
) are the ones that have their
in^{(t)}_{w}
increase in this iteration; thus, to avoid confusion, we use
w

in place of
v
in the following.

We first show that the total sum that can be assigned to
w
across the iterations is upper bounded by
\beta
in expectation. For technical reasons that will become clear shortly,
we prove this bound in a more general form (in the following claim, the statement above corresponds to setting
t^{\prime}=0
).

Claim 4.3
.

For any iteration
t^{\prime}\geqslant 1
, any
w\in V
, and choice of all the random bits
R^{<t^{\prime}}
before iteration
t^{\prime}
, we have,

\Exp\left[\sum_{t\geqslant t^{\prime}}in^{(t)}_{w}\mid R^{<t^{\prime}}\right]\leqslant\beta.

Proof.

We know that the given choice of random bits before iteration
t^{\prime}
,
R^{<t^{\prime}}
fixes the graph
G^{(t^{\prime})}
. If
w
is no longer present in
G^{(t^{\prime})}
,
in^{(t^{\prime})}_{w}=0
, and it remains zero for every iteration after
t^{\prime}
also. Hence, proving the statement is trivial. Thus, we assume that the vertex
w
is present in
G^{(t^{\prime})}
.

For any iteration
t\geqslant t^{\prime}
, and any choice of random bits
R^{<t}
for all preceding iterations,

\displaystyle\Exp\left[in^{(t)}_{w}\mid R^{<t}\right]

\displaystyle=\hskip-4.0pt\sum_{v\in N^{(t)}(w)}\hskip-4.0pt\mathbb{I}\left[\deg^{(t)}(v)\geqslant\deg^{(t)}(w)\right]\cdot\Prob\left(v\in V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}^{(t)}\mid R^{<t}\right)\cdot\frac{\beta}{\deg^{(t)}(v)}

(by the definition of the algorithm)

\displaystyle\leqslant\deg^{(t)}(w)\cdot\frac{\deg^{(t)}(v)}{n^{(t)}}\cdot\frac{\beta}{\deg^{(t)}(v)}

(as
v
belongs to
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}^{(t)}
if
u^{(t)}
belongs to
N^{(t)}(v)
)

\displaystyle=\beta\cdot\frac{\deg^{(t)}(w)}{n^{(t)}}.

On the other hand, for every vertex
w\in G^{(t)}
define the event:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{w}(t):\text{vertex $w$ is removed from $G^{(t)}$ by being picked as $u^{(t)}$ or joining $V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}^{(t)}$}.

We have,

\Prob\left(\mathcal{E}_{w}(t)\mid R^{<t}\right)=\Prob\left(u^{(t)}\in\left\{w\right\}\cup N^{(t)}(w)\right)=\frac{\deg^{(t)}(w)+1}{n^{(t)}}.

Thus, we have that

\displaystyle\Exp\left[in^{(t)}_{w}\mid R^{<t}\right]\leqslant\beta\cdot\Prob\left(\mathcal{E}_{w}(t)\mid R^{<t}\right).

(11)

Intuitively, this means that if
in^{(t)}_{w}
is expected to be “large”, then there is also a “good chance” that
w
is removed from
G^{(t)}
at this iteration and thus no longer receives
in^{(t)}_{w}
in the subsequent iterations.
We formalize this as follows:

\displaystyle\Exp\left[\sum_{t\geqslant t^{\prime}}in^{(t)}_{w}\mid R^{<t^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geqslant t^{\prime}}\sum_{R^{<t}}\mathbb{I}\left[\text{$w$ belongs to $G^{(t)}$ after $R^{<t}\mid R^{<t^{\prime}}$}\right]\cdot\Prob\left(R^{<t}\mid R^{<t^{\prime}}\right)\cdot\Exp\left[in^{(t)}_{w}\mid R^{<t},R^{<t^{\prime}}\right]

(by the law of total expectation)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t\geqslant t^{\prime}}\sum_{R^{<t}}\mathbb{I}\left[\text{$w$ belongs to $G^{(t)}$ after $R^{<t}\mid R^{<t^{\prime}}$}\right]\cdot\Prob\left(R^{<t}\mid R^{<t^{\prime}}\right)\cdot\beta\cdot\Prob\left(\mathcal{E}_{w}(t)\mid R^{<t},R^{<t^{\prime}}\right)

(by
Equation

11
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geqslant t^{\prime}}\Prob\left(\text{$w$ belongs to $G^{(t^{\prime})},\ldots,G^{(t)}$ but not $G^{(t+1)}\mid R^{<t^{\prime}}$}\right)\cdot\beta

(by the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{w}(t)
and the probability in the inner sum)

\displaystyle=\Prob\left(\text{$w$ is removed from some $G^{(t)}$ with $t\geqslant t^{\prime}$}\right)\cdot\beta

(as the events in the sum are mutually exclusive and partition the space)

\displaystyle=\beta,

(as
w
will be removed at some iteration after
t^{\prime}
)

concluding the proof.

We can also observe that the value of
in^{(t)}_{w}
in any iteration
t
is upper bounded by
\beta
.

Observation 4.4
.

For every
t\geqslant 1
, regardless of the choice of
R^{<t}
,
in^{(t)}_{w}\leqslant\beta
.

Proof.

We have,

in^{(t)}_{w}\leqslant\sum_{v\in N^{(t)}(w)}\mathbb{I}\left[\deg^{(t)}(v)\geqslant\deg^{(t)}(w)\right]\cdot\frac{\beta}{\deg^{(t)}(v)}\leqslant\deg^{(t)}(w)\cdot\frac{\beta}{\deg^{(t)}(w)}=\beta.\penalty\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 15.00002pt\penalty\vrule height=7.5pt,width=5.0pt,depth=2.5pt

Lastly, using
Observation

4.4
and
Claim

4.3
, we can show that the variance of the total value assigned to
w
is also low, enabling us to prove the required concentration bounds later.

Claim 4.5
.

For every
w\in V
,

\textnormal{Var}\left[\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{w}\right]\leqslant 3\beta\cdot\Exp\left[\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{w}\right].

Proof.

We can write the variance as,

\displaystyle\textnormal{Var}\left[\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{w}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geqslant 1}\textnormal{Var}\left[in^{(t)}_{w}\right]+2\cdot\sum_{t_{1}<t_{2}}\textnormal{Cov}\left[in^{(t_{1})}_{w},in^{(t_{2})}_{w}\right]

(as
\textnormal{Var}\left[\sum_{i}X_{i}\right]=\sum_{i}\textnormal{Var}\left[X_{i}\right]+\sum_{i\neq j}\textnormal{Cov}\left[X_{i},X_{j}\right]
for any random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t\geqslant 1}\Exp\left[(in^{(t)}_{w})^{2}\right]+2\cdot\sum_{t_{1}<t_{2}}\Exp\left[in^{(t_{1})}_{w}\cdot in^{(t_{2})}_{w}\right]

(as
\textnormal{Var}\left[X\right]\leqslant\Exp\left[X^{2}\right]
for any random variable
X
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\beta\cdot\sum_{t\geqslant 1}\Exp\left[in^{(t)}_{w}\right]+2\cdot\sum_{t_{1}<t_{2}}\Exp\left[in^{(t_{1})}_{w}\cdot in^{(t_{2})}_{w}\right]

(since by
Observation

4.4
, we have
in^{(t)}_{w}\leqslant\beta
)

We will bound the second term by
2\beta\cdot\Exp\left[\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{w}\right]
to complete the proof.

\displaystyle\sum_{t_{1}<t_{2}}\Exp\left[in^{(t_{1})}_{w}\cdot in^{(t_{2})}_{w}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t_{1}}\sum_{t_{2}\geqslant t_{1}+1}\Exp\left[in^{(t_{1})}_{w}\cdot in^{(t_{2})}_{w}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t_{1}}\sum_{R^{<t_{1}+1}}\Prob\left(R^{<t_{1}+1}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{t_{2}\geqslant t_{1}+1}\Exp\left[in^{(t_{1})}_{w}\cdot in^{(t_{2})}_{w}\mid R^{<t_{1}+1}\right]\right)

(by the law of total expectation)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t_{1}}\sum_{R^{<t_{1}+1}}\Prob\left(R^{<t_{1}+1}\right)\cdot\Exp\left[in^{(t_{1})}_{w}\mid R^{<t_{1}+1}\right]\cdot\left(\sum_{t_{2}\geqslant t_{1}+1}\Exp\left[in^{(t_{2})}_{w}\mid R^{<t_{1}+1}\right]\right)

(as the value of
in^{(t_{1})}_{w}
is fixed when conditioned on
R^{<t_{1}+1}
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t_{1}}\sum_{R^{<t_{1}+1}}\Prob\left(R^{<t_{1}+1}\right)\cdot\Exp\left[in^{(t_{1})}_{w}\mid R^{<t_{1}+1}\right]\cdot\beta

(by
Claim

4.3
)

\displaystyle=\beta\cdot\sum_{t_{1}}\Exp\left[in^{(t_{1})}_{w}\right]

(by the law of total expectation)

\displaystyle=\beta\cdot\Exp\left[\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{w}\right],

(by the linearity of expectation)

finishing the proof.

We use
Claim

4.5
to bound the probability that
in^{(t)}_{w}
reaches a “too large” value.

Claim 4.6
.

For every
w\in V
and every
\theta\geqslant 1-\beta
,

\Prob\left(\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{w}>\theta\right)\leqslant\frac{3\beta}{(\theta-\beta)^{2}}\cdot\Exp\left[\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{w}\right].

Proof.

Let
X:=\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{w}
. Note that
X
is a sum of at most
n
random variables as there are at most
n
iterations in
Algorithm

1
(in each iteration, at least one vertex is removed).
We have,

\displaystyle\Prob\left(\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{w}>\theta\right)

\displaystyle=\Prob\left(X>\theta\right)\leqslant\frac{\textnormal{Var}\left[X\right]}{(\theta-\Exp\left[X\right])^{2}}

(by Chebyshev’s inequality)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{3\beta\cdot\Exp\left[X\right]}{(\theta-\Exp\left[X\right])^{2}}

(by
Claim

4.5
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{3\beta}{(\theta-\beta)^{2}}\cdot\Exp\left[\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{w}\right].

(as
\Exp\left[X\right]=\Exp\left[\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{w}\right]\leqslant\beta<\theta
, by
Claim

4.3
and since
\beta<1/8
)

This proves the claim.

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

4.2
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.2
.

For every vertex
v\in V
, define

\textnormal{extra}{(v)}:=\min\left(\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{v}-(1-\beta),0\right).

We first have

\displaystyle\Exp\left[\textnormal{extra}{(v)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta=(1-\beta)}^{\infty}\Prob\left(\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{v}=\theta\right)\cdot(\theta-(1-\beta))

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\theta=(1-\beta)}^{\infty}\Prob\left(\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{v}=\theta\right)\cdot\theta

(as
\beta<1/8
and thus removing
-(1-\beta)
can only increase the sum)

\displaystyle=\int_{\theta=(1-\beta)}^{\infty}\Prob\left(\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{v}\geqslant\theta\right)\cdot d\theta

(as for any non-negative variable
X
,
\Exp\left[X\right]=\int_{0}^{\infty}(1-F_{X}(x))dx
where
F_{X}(\cdot)
is the CDF function)

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{\theta=(1-\beta)}^{\infty}\frac{3\beta}{(\theta-\beta)^{2}}\cdot\Exp\left[\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{v}\right]\cdot d\theta

(by
Claim

4.6
)

\displaystyle=3\beta\cdot\Exp\left[\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{v}\right]\cdot\int_{\theta=(1-\beta)}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(\theta-\beta)^{2}}d\theta

\displaystyle=3\beta\cdot\Exp\left[\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{v}\right]\cdot\frac{1}{1-2\beta}.

(as
\int 1/x^{2}dx=-1/x+
constant)

Finally, we have,

\displaystyle\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in E}y_{e}\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in E}x_{e}\right]-\sum_{v\in V}\Exp\left[\textnormal{extra}{(v)}\right].

(by the update in Line (
3
) and
Equation

10
)

\displaystyle\geqslant\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in E}x_{e}\right]-\frac{3\beta}{(1-2\beta)}\cdot\sum_{v\in V}\Exp\left[\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{v}\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\left(\frac{1-8\beta}{1-2\beta}\right)\cdot\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in E}x_{e}\right]

(as
\sum_{v\in V}\Exp\left[\sum_{t\geqslant 1}in^{(t)}_{v}\right]\leqslant 2\cdot\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in E}x_{e}\right]
)

concluding the proof.

The proof of
Theorem

2
now follows from
Lemma

4.1
for
Equation

6
and
Lemma

4.2
for
Equation

7
.

4.2
The Dynamic Streaming Implementation of
Algorithm

1

We now show how to implement
Algorithm

1
in dynamic streams. For this, we follow the approach of
[
ACG+15
]
for implementing the randomized greedy MIS algorithm,
which also forms the backbone of
Algorithm

1
. The main new step here is to find the assignments
x
and
y
to the edges of the graph in the algorithm (although we will not be able to explicitly find these, but rather a “proxy” to them).
To do this, we need a procedure that can determine the
exact
iteration each vertex is being removed from the graph. This is done via
Algorithm

2
that we design.

Algorithm

2
finds the set
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
in
Algorithm

1
, and assigns a
time stamp
to each
vertex that indicates in which iteration of the while-loop this vertex was removed, namely, was
settled
. This algorithm, similar to
[
ACG+15
]
, processes the
graph in
O(\log\log{n})

batches
of vertices with growing sizes. A key new subroutine allows us to determine the time stamp of
all
vertices.

Algorithm 2
.

An input graph
G=(V,E)
in a dynamic stream.

1.

Set the
time

t=0
and let
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}=\emptyset
and
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}=\emptyset
. Let
\sigma
be a random permutation of
V
.

2.

For
i=1
to
\log\log{n}

batches
:

(a)

Let
k_{i}:=2\cdot(n^{1-1/2^{i}}-n^{1-1/2^{i-1}})
and
U_{i}:=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k_{i}})
be the next
k_{i}
vertices in
\sigma
to be processed
14
14

14

If the remaining graph has
<k_{i}
vertices, we let
U_{i}
be all remaining vertices.
.

(b)

In a
single pass
over the stream, store
G[U_{i}\setminus(V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}\cup V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}})]
using
Claim

4.9
below.

(c)

At the end of the pass:
for

j=1
to
k_{i}

do
the following:

i.

If
u_{j}
is
settled
already, move to the next vertex of the for-loop in Line (
2c
).

ii.

Else, increase
t\leftarrow t+1
and add
u_{j}
to
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
with
t(u_{j})=t
and mark neighbors of
u_{j}
in
U_{i}\setminus(V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}\cup V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}})
as settled.

(d)

In a
single pass
over the stream, for every
v\in V\setminus V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
, find the vertex
u\in N(v)\cap V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
with the smallest value of
t(u)

using
Algorithm

3
(to be defined in
Section

4.2.1
); if such a vertex
u
is found for
v
, add
v
to
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
, set
t(v)=t(u)
, and mark
v
as settled.

We start by arguing that this algorithm faithfully simulates
Algorithm

1
.

Observation 4.7
.

For any graph
G=(V,E)
, the set
\left\{(v,t(v))\mid v\in V\right\}
in
Algorithm

2
is sampled from the same distribution as in
Algorithm

1
where
t(v)
in the latter refers
to the iteration of the while-loop wherein
v
joins
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
or is picked as the vertex
u
(i.e., is removed from the graph).

Proof.

In
Algorithm

1
, we can think of sampling a vertex
u
uniformly from the remaining graph in each iteration of the while-loop, as first sampling a random permutation
\sigma
, and
then picking the remaining vertex of
G
with the smallest value of
\sigma
in each iteration of the while-loop. Moreover, we have that,

\sum_{i=1}^{\log\log{n}}k_{i}=2\cdot n^{1-1/2^{\log\log{n}}}=\frac{2n}{n^{1/\log{n}}}=n,

and thus any remaining vertex will be sampled in the last iteration of the algorithm and will be processed. Then, it is easy to verify that the two algorithms are performing the same
exact computation, finalizing the proof.

An important remark is in order here. Given the greedy nature of
Algorithm

1
, for the purpose of the analysis (and by using
Observation

4.7
), we can also consider the choice of vertices
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
right after
we process a vertex
u
in
Algorithm

2
(to be added to
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
), even though in reality, vertices in
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
are only added after the batch is fully processed. In other words, in the analysis, we can add neighbors of
u
to
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}

right at that point even though these vertices will be added to
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
at the end of the batch.

Let us define some useful notation about the random variables in
Algorithm

2
before we proceed.

\displaystyle G_{i}

\displaystyle:\text{the graph $G[U_{i}\setminus(V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}\cup V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}})]$ that is stored in batch $i$};

\displaystyle n_{i}

\displaystyle:=2n^{1-1/2^{i}}=\sum_{j=1}^{i}k_{j},\text{ which is the number of vertices in all of $U_{1}\cup U_{2}\cup\ldots\cup U_{i}$};

\displaystyle U_{\leqslant i}

\displaystyle:\text{the set of $n_{i}$ vertices in $U_{1}\cup U_{2}\cup\ldots\cup U_{i}$};

\displaystyle N_{i}(w)

\displaystyle:\text{for any vertex $w\in G_{i}$, this is the neighborhood of $w$ in graph $G_{i}$}.

(We emphasize that in the above notation,
n_{i}
is
not
the number of vertices in
G_{i}
and is larger.)

We prove a helper lemma that allows us to bound the space complexity of this algorithm in Line (
2b
). This is a standard result at this point—originally due to
[
ACG+15
]
—and is often
referred to as the “residual sparsity property” of the greedy (MIS) algorithm
[
GGK+18
,
Kon18
,
AOSS19
]
. We thus provide the proof only for completeness.

Lemma 4.8

(cf.
[
ACG+15
]
)
.

For
1<i\leqslant\log\log n
, let
\Delta(G_{i})
denote the maximum degree of
G_{i}
. Then, with high probability,

\Delta(G_{i})\leqslant 100\cdot(n_{i}/n_{i-1})\cdot\ln{n}.

Proof.

We prove the statement for
i+1
with
i\geqslant 1
.
Fix the
n_{i+1}
vertices in
U_{\leqslant i+1}
, but
not
their order in the permutation
\sigma
.
We can think of sampling the set
U_{\leqslant i}
as picking
n_{i}
vertices
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n_{i}}
uniformly at random from the set
U_{\leqslant i+1}
one at a time (without replacement). Now, consider a vertex
v
in
U_{\leqslant i+1}

and for
j\in[n_{i}]
, let
d_{j}(v)
denote the degree of
v
in
G[U_{\leqslant i+1}\setminus(V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}^{j}\cup V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}^{j})]
where
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}^{j}\cup V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}^{j}
includes the part of
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
and
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
that will be added due to the
choices of vertices
u_{1},\ldots,u_{j-1}
and their neighbors. Finally, let
d(v)

denote the degree of
v
in
G_{i+1}
.
We have,

\displaystyle\Prob\left(v\in G_{i+1}\wedge d(v)\geqslant 100\cdot\frac{n_{i+1}}{n_{i}}\cdot\ln{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\prod_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\Prob\left(\text{$u_{j}$ is not in current neighbors of $v$}\mid u_{1},\ldots,u_{j-1}\right)

(as
v
remaining in
G_{i+1}
means
none of its neighbors are sampled in
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\prod_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\left(1-\frac{d_{j}(v)}{n_{i+1}}\right)

(as each
u_{j}
is chosen without replacement from
n_{i+1}-1-(j-1)
vertices at this point)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(1-\frac{100\cdot(n_{i+1}/n_{i})\cdot\ln{n}}{n_{i+1}}\right)^{n_{i}}

(as
d_{j}(v)\geqslant d(v)
since the degrees drop monotonically as
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
and
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
grow)

\displaystyle\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{n_{i}\cdot 100\cdot\ln{n}}{n_{i}}\right)=n^{-100}.

(as
1-x\leqslant e^{-x}
)

A union bound over all the vertices concludes the proof.

Claim 4.9
.

Line (
2b
) of
Algorithm

2
can be implemented in
O(n\log^{2}{n})
space with high probability.

Proof.

The statement holds vacuously for
i=1
, because the total number of vertices in
G_{1}
is
k_{1}=n_{1}=2\sqrt{n}
; hence, we can store a counter between all pairs of vertices in
G_{1}

during the stream and recover all the edges in
O(n\log n)
space trivially.

Consider each batch
1<i\leqslant\log\log{n}
of the algorithm. By
Lemma

4.8
, with high probability, we can bound the maximum degree of the graph
G_{i}
. We
We also know that the total number of vertices stored in batch
i
is
k_{i}\leqslant n_{i}=2n^{1-1/2^{i}}
. Thus, the total number of edges in
G_{i}
is at most,

n_{i}\cdot\Delta(G_{i})\leqslant 100\cdot(n_{i}^{2}/n_{i-1})\cdot\ln{n}=100\cdot(4n^{2-2/2^{i}}/2n^{1-2^{i-1}})\cdot\ln{n}=200\cdot n\cdot\ln{n},

(12)

where the first inequality is by
Lemma

4.8
and the second equality is by the choice of
n_{i}
and
n_{i-1}
.

We now run a sparse recovery algorithm to recover all edges of
G[U_{i}\setminus(V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}\cup V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}})]
in Line (
2b
). Specifically, define
\phi\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{m}
to be
the indicator vector of edges of this subgraph. Given that the algorithm explicitly stores
U_{i},V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}},
and
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
,
we can define
\phi
on the fly when seeing the updates to the edges of
G
.

The total number of non-zero elements of
\phi
is
O(n\log n)
, by
Equation

12
. Thus, we can run the deterministic sparse-recovery of
Proposition

3.6
with a single pass
over
\phi
, and the choice of
q=O(n\log{n})
using
O(n\log^{2}{n})
bits of space. This concludes implementation of Line (
2b
).

4.2.1
Finding Time Stamps in
Algorithm

2

We now show how to find the time stamps in Line (
2d
) of
Algorithm

2
. We devise the following algorithm for each batch
i\in[\log\log{n}]
.
Note that at this point, the algorithm has computed
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
and their time stamp for the current batch
i
but have not done so for
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
and that is the task of the following algorithm.

Algorithm 3
.

For implementing Line (
2d
) of
Algorithm

2
in each batch
i\in[\log\log{n}]
.

1.

Partition the set
U_{i}
into
b=\log{k_{i}}
groups based on geometrically increasing sizes,
namely, for every
j\in[b]
,
U_{i,j}
contains the next
2^{j}
elements of
U_{i}
in the order of permutation
\sigma
:
15
15

15

The last group
b
may contain less than
2^{b}
elements, as it only has all the remaining elements of
U_{i}
.

U_{i,j}=\left\{u_{2^{j}-1},u_{2^{i}},\ldots,u_{2^{j+1}-2}\right\}.

2.

For every
v\in V
and
j\in[b]
, define the vector
\phi(v,j)\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{V}
as the indicator vector of
N(v)\cap U_{i,j}\cap V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
.
Note that by the end of Line (
2c
) of
Algorithm

2
, the set
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}\cap U_{i,j}
is known and each update
(u,v)
to the dynamic stream for edges of the input graph
G
can be used to also update the vectors
\phi(u,j)
and
\phi(v,j)
for all
j\in[b]
.

3.

Let
q:=200\ln{n}
and for every
v\in V
and
j\in[b]
, run a randomized
q
-sparse recovery algorithm of
Proposition

3.6
on
\phi(v,j)
and test if
\phi(v,j)
is
q
-sparse or not, using
\delta=1/n^{200}
.

4.

Let
j\in[b]
be the smallest index such that
Proposition

3.6
declares
0<\|\phi(v,j)\|_{0}\leqslant q
and returns
\phi(v,j)
; let
u\in V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
be the vertex with the minimum
t(u)
in the support of
\phi(v,j)
.

Return
(u,t(u))
as the choice for the vertex
v
in Line (
2d
) of
Algorithm

2
(if no such
j
is found for
v
, return no
u
exists for
v
).

Lemma 4.10
.

With high probability,
Algorithm

3
finds the time-stamps in
Line (
2d
) of
Algorithm

2
and can be implemented in
O(n\log^{3}{n})
space.

Proof.

We will show that for any vertex
v\in V\setminus V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
,
Algorithm

3
finds the required time-stamp
t(v)
with high probability.

Consider the smallest index
j^{\star}\in[b]
where
\|\phi(v,j^{\star})\|_{0}>0
; if no such
j^{\star}
exists then
v
is not a neighbor to any vertex in
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
and thus will not be added to
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
. Otherwise,
the neighbor
u
of
v
in
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
with the smallest value of
t(u)
belongs to
U_{i,j^{\star}}
. Thus, returning such
u
is the correct answer.

Let us see how the algorithm performs for any
j\in[b]
, and enumerate the sources of error.

(i)

For any
j<j^{\star}
, we know that
\|\phi(v,j)\|_{0}=0
, but the randomized sparse-recovery may return that
\|\phi(v,j)\|_{0}>q
with probability at most
\delta
.

(ii)

For
j=j^{\star}
, it may be the case that
\|\phi(v,j)\|_{0}>q
or if
\|\phi(v,j)\|_{0}\leqslant q
, but randomized sparse-recovery returns otherwise.

Firstly, let us condition on the fact that randomized sparse-recovery performs correctly and retrieves
\phi(v,j)
for all
v\in V\setminus V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
and
j\in[b]
.
Using union bound over all vertices and
j\in[b]
, we get that this event happens with probability at least
1-n^{-198}
for the choice of
\delta=n^{-200}
.

Now, we only need to bound the probability that the case
\|\phi(v,j^{\star})_{0}\|>q
happens. We do so using the following intermediate claim.

Claim 4.11
.

For any
j>1
and any vertex
v\in V\setminus V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
, if
\|\phi(v,j-1)\|_{0}=0
, then
\|\phi(v,j)\|_{0}\leqslant q
with high probability.

Proof.

Let us fix the vertices
U_{i}
which are picked to be in
\sigma
, but not the order in which they are processed, i.e., we know the
k_{i}
vertices that belong to
U_{i}
but not the vertex groupings of
U_{i,j}
for
j\in[b]
.
We define some useful notation to prove this claim.

\displaystyle G_{i,j}

\displaystyle:\text{the graph $G[U_{i}\setminus(V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}\cup V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}})]$ at the beginning of sampling $U_{i,j}$, where, $V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}\cup V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}$ }

\displaystyle\text{\hskip 11.38109ptinclude all the vertices in $U_{i}$ settled by all the vertices in $\sigma$ till $U_{i,j-1}$};

\displaystyle d_{j}(v)

\displaystyle:\text{the degree of $v$ in $G_{i,j}$};

\displaystyle k_{i,j}

\displaystyle:k_{i}-(2^{1}+2^{2}+\ldots+2^{j-1}),\text{ the number of vertices left in $U_{i}$ after}

U_{i,1},U_{i,2},\ldots,U_{i,j-1}
are sampled

For any
j<b
with
2^{j}\leqslant k_{i,j}
(group
j
is not the last group), we can think of picking
U_{i,j}
as sampling
2^{j}
vertices from the
k_{i,j}
vertices of
U_{i}\setminus(U_{i,1}\cup U_{i,2}\cup\ldots\cup U_{i,j-1})
without replacement. Each vertex is picked in
U_{i,j}
with probability
p_{j}
, where,

p_{j}:=2^{j}/k_{i,j}.

(13)

Suppose
d_{j}(v)<100\ln{n}/p_{j}
. Then, in expectation, we know that there are at most
100\ln n
neighbors of
v
in
G\setminus(V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}\cup V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}})
. We use Chernoff bound for negatively correlated random variables in
Proposition

3.2
(because of sampling without replacement) to argue that
with high probability, we cannot have more than
200\ln{n}=q
neighbors of
v
in
G\setminus(V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}\cup V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}})
that are sampled in
U_{i,j}
; even if all of those neighbors join
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
,
we will still have that sparsity of
\phi(v,j)
is at most
q
as desired.

Now we need to argue the case when
d_{j}(v)\geqslant 100\ln{n}/p_{j}
. This implies that,

\displaystyle d_{j}(v)

\displaystyle\geqslant 100\ln{n}/p_{j}

(as the set
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}\cup V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
only grows)

\displaystyle=100\ln{n}\cdot\frac{k_{i,j}}{2^{j}}

(by value of
p_{j}
in
Equation

13
)

\displaystyle=50\ln{n}\cdot\frac{k_{i,j}}{2^{j-1}}

\displaystyle=50\ln{n}\cdot\frac{1}{p_{j-1}}\cdot\frac{k_{i,j}}{k_{i,j-1}}.

(given that
p_{j-1}=2^{j-1}/k_{i,j-1}
)

\displaystyle=50\ln{n}\cdot\frac{1}{p_{j-1}}\cdot\frac{k_{i,j-1}-2^{j-1}}{k_{i,j-1}}

(by definition of
k_{i,j}
)

\displaystyle=50\ln{n}\cdot\frac{1}{p_{j-1}}\cdot(1-p_{j-1})

\displaystyle\geqslant 25\ln{n}\cdot(1/p_{j-1}).

(
k_{i,j-1}\geqslant 2^{j}+2^{j-1}\geqslant 2\cdot 2^{j-1}
, we get
p_{j-1}\leqslant 1/2
)

When group
j
is the last group with
2^{j}\geqslant k_{i,j}
(here
p_{j}=1
), we have,

d_{j}(v)\geqslant 100\ln{n}\geqslant 25\ln{n}\cdot(1/p_{j-1}),

where we have used that
k_{i,j-1}<2^{j-1}+2^{j}<4\cdot 2^{j-1}
, and
p_{j-1}\geqslant 1/4
.

We have argued that
d_{j}(v)\geqslant 25\ln{n}/p_{j-1}
in all cases. Among these
d_{j}(v)
vertices, none of them are sampled in
U_{i,j-1}
as
v
is not settled in
U_{i,j}
. However, in expectation,
25\ln{n}
of these vertices must have been sampled in
U_{i,j-1}
.
We use the same argument as in
Lemma

4.8
to say that at least one of these
d_{j}(v)
vertices will be sampled in
U_{i,j-1}
and
v
will have a neighbor in
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
already inside
U_{i,j-1}
, with high probability. This contradicts our assumption that
\|\phi(v,j-1)\|_{0}=0
.

Thus, in this case
j
cannot be the first index with non-empty support in
\phi(v,j)
.

Claim

4.11

To finalize the proof, we have that if
j^{\star}=1
,
U_{i,1}
has only 2 vertices and
\phi(v,1)
is
2
-sparse; for any
j^{\star}>1
, we use
Claim

4.11
to argue that for any vertex
v
,
\phi(v,j^{\star})
is
q
-sparse with high probability.
This proves the correctness of the algorithm as argued earlier.

We can now bound the space of the algorithm. For every
v\in V
, we are maintaining
O(\log{n})
randomized sparse-recovery algorithms, each for recovering an
O(\log{n})
-sparse vector from a domain of size
\leqslant n
and error probability
\delta=1/\mbox{\rm poly}(n)
; thus,
by
Proposition

3.6
, this needs
O(\log^{3}{n})
space per vertex, and
O(n\log^{3}{n})
space in total, concluding the proof of
Lemma

4.10
.

4.2.2
Finding a Large Matching from
Algorithm

2
via the Reduction of
Algorithm

1

Algorithm

2
allows us to recover the time stamps of all vertices in a single run of the randomized greedy MIS algorithm in
O(\log\log{n})
passes over a dynamic stream.
We now use this information alongside our reduction of approximate matching to randomized greedy MIS in
Algorithm

1
, to recover a large matching from the input graph.

Let us first recall some notation about some variables in
Algorithm

1
:

\displaystyle G^{(t)}

\displaystyle:\text{the graph $G$ at iteration $t$};

\displaystyle\deg^{(t)}(v)

\displaystyle:\text{for each $v\in G^{(t)}$, denotes the degree of $v$ in $G^{(t)}$};

\displaystyle x_{e}

\displaystyle:\text{the value given to edge $e$ in $x\in\mathbb{R}^{E}$ from \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{eq:random-order-1}}

\displaystyle y_{e}

\displaystyle:\text{the value given to edge $e$ in fractional matching $y\in[0,1]^{E}$ from \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{eq:final-apx-ye}}.

As we have stated earlier, given the support of the fractional matching
y
can be too large, we cannot hope to recover it explicitly. Instead,
our goal is to
sample
the edges of the graph with probabilities proportional to their
y
-values, and then use the sampled edges to find a large matching.
Specifically, for every
e\in E
, define:

p_{e}:=\min(1,y_{e}\cdot 200\log n).

(14)

We would like to sample each edge of the graph with probability
p_{e}
. The challenge however is that we will not be able to actually recover the values of
y
(or even
x
) explicitly and learn
p_{e}
’s, and thus need to use a “proxy” for them algorithmically.

To start with, we have the time stamps of all the vertices. Let us show that this also gives us the degrees of all the vertices at the time in which they are settled.

Observation 4.12
.

Given the time stamps
t(v)
for all
v\in V
, using one pass and
O(n\log{n})
space, we can find
\deg^{(t(v))}(v)
for all vertices
v\in V
.

Proof.

For any vertex
v
,
\deg^{(t(v))}(v)
only includes the edges of
v
to vertices
w\in V
which have
t(w)\geqslant t(v)
. We know the labels of all vertices explicitly, so it is easy to count the total number of edges from
v
to vertices with labels after
v

using a counter per vertex.

Equipped with
Observation

4.12
, we can assume we also have the remaining degree of every vertex
v\in V
at the time it is settled. This fixes the value
x
will assign to the edges of
v
.
The problem however is that we still do not have sufficient information to perform the check in Line (
2c
) of
Algorithm

1
to know which incident edges of
v
receive
a non-zero fractional matching, i.e., which edges
(v,w)
satisfy
\deg(w)\leqslant\deg(v)
; this is because for this check, we need to know the degree of
w
at the time
v
is being settled not
w
itself.

We side step this issue in the following by defining an intermediate assignment
z\in\mathbb{R}^{E}
which we can explicitly find for any pair of vertices.
To formally define the vector
z
, we describe the notion of assigning a vertex pair to one of the vertices.

Definition 4.13

(Assignment of vertex pairs to vertices)
.

For any pair of vertices
u,v
, we say the pair
u,v
is assigned to vertex
u
, denoted by
\textnormal{{vert}}(u,v)
, iff:

(i)

u
is settled before vertex
v
, i.e.,
t(u)<t(v)
, or else,

(ii)

u,v
are removed at the same time with
t(u)=t(v)
and
u\in V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
while
v\in V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
, or else,

(iii)

t(u)=t(v)
,
u,v\in V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
, and
\deg^{(t(u))}(u)\geqslant\deg^{(t(v))}(v)
(breaking the ties between
u
and
v
in case of the equality consistently, say, based on whichever appear in
\sigma
first).

Note that by
Definition

4.13
, every pair
u,v
of vertices, regardless of whether or not is an edge, is assigned to one of its endpoints.
We then define
z_{uv}
for a pair
u,v\in V
as,

z_{uv}:=\frac{200\log n}{\deg^{(t(\textnormal{{vert}}(u,v)))}(\textnormal{{vert}}(u,v))};

(15)

that is
z_{uv}
is proportional to the inverse of the degree of its assigned vertex, at the time this assigned vertex was settled.
When vertices
u,v
do contain an edge
e=(u,v)
in the input graph, we use
z_{e}
and
\textnormal{{vert}}(e)
also to denote
z_{uv}
and
\textnormal{{vert}}(u,v)
interchangeably.

We observe that for any vertex pair
u,v
, the value of
z_{uv}
can be found easily.

Observation 4.14
.

For any vertex pair
u,v
, the value of
z_{uv}
can be determined given the information collected by the algorithm.

Proof.

The only information required to find
\textnormal{{vert}}(e)
is the time stamps, the sets
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
and
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
, and the degrees at the time of removal, and lastly the permutation
\sigma
, all of which is stored in the memory explicitly (using
Lemma

4.10

and
Observation

4.12
).
Thus, we can find for each vertex pair what
\textnormal{{vert}}(u,v)
is, and then, determining
z_{uv}
is trivial.

The following claims shows the relevance of
z
-values for us.

Claim 4.15
.

For every edge
e\in E
,
p_{e}\leqslant z_{e}
where
p_{e}\in[0,1]
is from
Equation

14
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show that
x_{e}\cdot 200\log n\leqslant z_{e}
given that
y_{e}\leqslant x_{e}
always (
y
is obtained from
x
by reducing some of its values) and by the definition of
p_{e}
based on
y_{e}
in
Equation

14
.

For any edge
e=(u,v)\in E
, the value of
x_{e}
is updated in at most one iteration of the loop in Line (
2c
) of
Algorithm

1
, i.e., the iteration where either of
u
or
v
are settled. We will argue that

x_{e}\leqslant\frac{\beta}{\deg^{(t(\textnormal{{vert}}(e)))}(\textnormal{{vert}}(e))}.

We have multiple cases to consider:

•

When
v
is settled (strictly) before
u
, that is
t(v)<t(u)
, we have that
\textnormal{{vert}}(e)=v
from Line

(
i
)

of
Definition

4.13
. We know that
x_{e}
is set as
\beta/\deg^{(t(v))}(v)
if
\deg^{(t(v))}(u)\leqslant\deg^{(t(v))}(v)
, otherwise
x_{e}
is set as zero. In either case,
x_{e}
is less than
\beta/\deg^{(t(\textnormal{{vert}}(e)))}(\textnormal{{vert}}(e))
. This is similarly true when
u
is settled (strictly) before
v
.

•

When
v
and
u
are settled at the same time, with
t(u)=t(v)
, and if
u
goes to
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{mis}}}
at this iteration, then
v\in V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
, and
x_{e}
is again updated as
\beta/\deg^{(t(v))}(v)
. We have that
\textnormal{{vert}}(e)=v
by Line

(

⁢
i
i

)

of
Definition

4.13
and can apply the above reasoning.

•

When both
u,v
go to
V_{\textnormal{\tiny{cover}}}
and have the same timestamps, the value of
x_{e}
is set as
\beta/\deg^{(t(v))}(v)
if
v
is the higher degree vertex among
u,v
at time
t(u)=t(v)
. Again, we have that
\textnormal{{vert}}(e)=v
by Line

(

⁢
i
i
i

)

of
Definition

4.13
and can apply the above reasoning.

The claim follows by observing that,

x_{e}\cdot 200\cdot\log n\leqslant\frac{\beta\cdot 200\cdot\log n}{\deg^{(t(\textnormal{{vert}}(e)))}(\textnormal{{vert}}(e))}\leqslant\frac{200\cdot\log n}{\deg^{(t(\textnormal{{vert}}(e)))}(\textnormal{{vert}}(e))}=z_{e},

where we have used that
\beta<1
.

Given
Claim

4.15
, for algorithmic purposes, we can instead sample the edges with probability proportional to
z
-values, which we
can
compute.
We now need to check this can be done efficiently in dynamic streams (and that the size of the sampled edges are not too large, given
z
is
not
a fractional matching).
Our sampling is done in two steps: we first sample
all
pairs
u,v
based on their value
z_{u,v}
to obtain a vector
\phi_{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{{{V}\choose{2}}}
and then maintain a sparse-recovery algorithm over
\phi_{z}
to
recover the actual edges inside this sample. Furthermore, we need to sample
\phi_{z}
itself with
limited independence hash functions
(defined in
Section

3.1
)
and work with
\phi_{z}

implicitly
given that its size is larger than the allowed space. We now formalize this:

Sampling process.

Let
\kappa:=200\log{n}
and sample a
\kappa
-wise independent hash function from
Definition

3.3
with domain of the set of vertex pairs and the range of
[0,1]
16
16

16

For the simplicity of exposition, we let the range of the hash function to be the interval
[0,1]
. For algorithmic purposes, one needs to further discretize this range to integers. Specifically, use the range as
\{1,2,\ldots,\mbox{\rm poly}(n)\}
for some large polynomial in
n
and consider a mapping of
[0,1]
to this range in a standard way. The distribution when sampling from the hash family with this large discrete domain varies from the range
[0,1]

by at most
1/\mbox{\rm poly}(n)
in total variation distance, and this negligible difference can be added to the probability of error of the algorithm.
:

h:\binom{n}{2}\rightarrow[0,1].

(16)

Let
\Phi_{z}\subseteq{{V}\choose{2}}
be the set of pairs such that
h(u,v)\leqslant\min(1,z_{u,v})
and
\phi_{z}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\Phi_{z}}
be the incidence vector of
E_{z}:=E\cap\Phi_{z}
. This way, the elements in the support of
\phi_{z}

are obtained from
E
by sampling each edge
e\in E
, using a
\kappa
-wise independent hash function, with probability
z_{e}
.

We now analyze this sampling process. The size of
\Phi_{z}
may still be too large for us but we show that the total number of
actual edges
sampled, i.e., the support of
\phi_{z}
will not be large
and can be found and stored explicitly by the algorithm.

Claim 4.16
.

The set
E_{z}
can be found and stored in
O(n\log^{2}n)
space explicitly with high probability.

Proof.

We will prove that size of
E_{z}
is
O(n\log{n})
with high probability (over the randomness of
h
alone).
This will be sufficient to prove the claim since we can implicitly maintain the set
\Phi_{z}
using the hash function
h
, which itself can be stored in
O(\kappa\cdot\log{n})=O(\log^{2}{n})
space using
Proposition

3.4
(see also
Footnote

16
); then, we will store a randomized sparse-recovery of
Proposition

3.6
for the vector
\phi_{z}
and each relevant update on the graph
G
can be passed to this vector given the implicit access to
\Phi_{z}
. This way,
we can recover
E_{z}
, the support of
\phi_{z}
, in
O(n\log^{2}{n})
space with high probability.

It thus suffices to prove the bound on the size of
E_{z}
. For any vertex
v\in V
, let
A(v)
be the set of edges
e\in E
that are assigned to
v
, i.e.,
\textnormal{{vert}}(e)=v
.
We claim that
A(v)\cap E_{z}
is of size
O(\log{n})
with high probability which implies the bound on
E_{z}
immediately since the set
\left\{A(v)\mid v\in V\right\}
covers all edges of the graph.

Let us fix a vertex
v\in V
. Let
d_{v}=\deg^{(t(v))}(v)
be the degree of
v
at the time it is settled.
We argue that
\left|{A(v)}\right|\leqslant d_{v}
. This is because any edge
e=(v,w)
assigned to
v
satisfies
t(w)\geqslant t(v)
and thus belongs to the graph at the time
v
is settled.
If
d_{v}\leqslant 200\log{n}
, we are already done, so in the following we assume
d_{v}>200\log{n}
.

For
e\in A(v)
, define the indicator random variable
X_{e}\in\left\{0,1\right\}
which is
1
iff
e
is sampled in
A_{z}
. By
Equation

15
, we have
\Exp\left[X_{e}\right]=200\log{n}/d_{v}

and thus by the argument above

\Exp\left|{E_{z}\cap A(v)}\right|=\left|{A(v)}\right|\cdot\frac{200\log{n}}{d_{v}}\leqslant d_{v}\cdot\frac{200\log{n}}{d_{v}}=200\log{n}.

Moreover,
\left\{Z_{e}\right\}_{e\in A(v)}
are chosen via a
\kappa
-wise independent hash function. Thus, by
Proposition

3.5
,

\Prob\left(\left|{E_{z}\cap A(v)}\right|\geqslant 400\log{n}\right)\leqslant\exp\left(-\min\left(\frac{\kappa}{2},\frac{2}{3}\cdot 200\log{n}\right)\right)\leqslant n^{-100},

by the choice of
\kappa=200\log{n}
. A union bound over all the vertices concludes the proof.

We have proved that our sampling process does not violate the space constraint and we can obtain the set
E_{z}

as well. We now finalize the argument by showing that
E_{z}
contains a large matching in expectation by relating it to the fractional matching
y
of
Algorithm

1
.
This is a combination of the standard fact that sampling a fractional matching leads to a large matching as well (slightly modified to work even with limited-independence sampling), and a rejection sampling argument
in the analysis to relate sampling with probability
z_{e}
to the one with
p_{e}
needed for the fractional matching sampling.

Lemma 4.17
.

There exists a matching
M
in the sampled edges
E_{z}
with

\Exp\left|{M}\right|\geqslant\left(\frac{1-8\beta}{1-2\beta}\right)\cdot\frac{\beta}{10}\cdot\mu(G).

Proof.

Fix a choice of random ordering
\sigma
in
Algorithm

1
and its simulation in
Algorithm

2
and let
y\in\mathbb{R}^{E}
be the fractional matching defined by this process.
Let
R
denote the entire randomness of these algorithms, which we condition on in the following proof. Thus, the only randomness remained at this point is in the sampling process of
E_{z}
.

Consider any choice of the hash function
h
in the sampling process from earlier. Define the following alternative sampling process for obtaining a set
E_{p}
as opposed to
E_{z}
:
add any edge
e\in E
to
E_{p}
iff
h(e)\leqslant p_{e}
(recall
p_{e}=\min(1,200\log{n}\cdot y_{e})
by
Equation

14
). Since
p_{e}\leqslant z_{e}
by
Claim

4.15
, for any choice of
h
, the set
E_{p}\subseteq E_{z}
. We can thus think of
E_{p}
as
obtained from
E_{z}
by rejecting the edges with
p_{e}<h(e)\leqslant z_{e}
. We emphasize that we are not algorithmically finding
E_{p}
but rather only define it for the analysis. Thus, picking the random hash function
h

defines a sampling process for picking
E_{p}
as well.

We now argue that

\displaystyle\Exp\left[\mu(E_{p})\mid R\right]\geqslant\frac{1}{5}\left|{y}\right|,

(17)

where
\left|{y}\right|=\sum_{e\in E}y_{e}
. This is sufficient to conclude the proof since,

\displaystyle\Exp\left[\mu(E_{z})\right]

\displaystyle=\Exp_{R}\Big[\Exp\left[\mu(E_{z})\mid R\right]\Big]\geqslant\Exp_{R}\Big[\Exp\left[\mu(E_{p})\mid R\right]\Big]

(by the law of total expectation and since
\mu(E_{z})\geqslant\mu(E_{p})
given
E_{p}\subseteq E_{z}
)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{5}\cdot\Exp_{R}\Big[\Exp\left[\left|{y}\right|\mid R\right]\Big]=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\Exp\left|{y}\right|

(by
Equation

17
and the law of total expectation again)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{5}\cdot\left(\frac{1-8\beta}{1-2\beta}\right)\cdot\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\mu(G)\cdot\left(1-1/\mbox{\rm poly}{(n)}\right),

where the final inequality holds by
Theorem

2
for the choice of
y
in
Algorithm

1
with the additional
1-1/\mbox{\rm poly}{(n)}
accounting from the correctness probability
of
Algorithm

2
in
Lemma

4.10
.

It thus remains to prove
Equation

17
.
Define the following assignment
y^{*}_{e}
for each edge
e\in E
:

y^{*}_{e}:=\begin{cases}y_{e}\qquad&\textnormal{if $e\in E_{p}$ and $y_{e}\geqslant 1/(200\log n)$,}\\
y_{e}/p_{e}\qquad&\textnormal{if $e\in E_{p}$ and $y_{e}<1/(200\log n)$,}\\
0\qquad&\textnormal{otherwise.}\end{cases}

This way,
\Exp\left[y^{*}_{e}\mid R\right]=y_{e}
for all
e\in E
: if
y_{e}\geqslant 1/(200\log{n})
, we have
p_{e}=1
and thus
h(e)\leqslant p_{e}
always meaning
e\in E_{p}
and
y^{*}_{e}=y_{e}
deterministically.
Otherwise,
e
belongs to
E_{p}
with probability
p_{e}<1
and thus
\Exp\left[y^{*}_{e}\right]=p_{e}\cdot y_{e}/p_{e}=p_{e}
.

We have

\Exp\left[\sum_{e\in E}y^{*}_{e}\mid R\right]=\sum_{e\in E}y_{e}=\left|{y}\right|,

and at the same time
y^{*}
is only supported on the edges in
E_{p}
. We are going to prove that, with a proper scaling,
y^{*}
can actually become a fractional matching. This means
E_{p}
contains a
fractional matching with expected size
\approx\left|{y}\right|
which will be sufficient to prove
Equation

17
.

Fix a vertex
v\in V
and define,

\displaystyle y^{\textnormal{fix}}_{v}:=\sum_{e\ni v}y_{e}\cdot\mathbb{I}(y_{e}\geqslant 1/(200\log n))\quad\text{and}\quad y^{*}_{v}:=\sum_{e\ni v}y^{*}_{e}\cdot\mathbb{I}(y_{e}<1/(200\log n)).

It is easy to see that
y^{\textnormal{fix}}_{v}\leqslant 1
as
y\in\mathbb{R}^{E}
is a fractional matching. Define
E_{p}(v)
as the edges in
E_{p}
that are incident on
v
.
We can also observe that,

\displaystyle y^{*}_{v}=\sum_{e\ni v}y^{*}_{e}\cdot\mathbb{I}(y_{e}<1/(200\log n))

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{200\log n}\cdot\left|{E_{p}(v)}\right|.

(18)

because, when
y_{e}<1/(200\log n)
, we know that
y_{e}/p_{e}=1/(200\log n)
. We have,

\Exp\left[\left|{E_{p}(v)}\right|\mid R\right]=\sum_{e\ni v}p_{e}=\sum_{e\ni v}y_{e}\cdot(200\log{n})\leqslant 200\log{n},

again using the fact that
y
is a fractional matching. Moreover,
\left|{E_{p}(v)}\right|
is a sum of
\kappa
-wise independent random variables (by the choice of
h
) and thus, by
Proposition

3.5
,

\Prob\left(\left|{E_{p}(v)}\right|\geqslant 400\log{n}\mid R\right)\leqslant\exp\left(-\min\left(\frac{\kappa}{2},\frac{2}{3}\cdot 200\log{n}\right)\right)\leqslant n^{-100},

by the choice of
\kappa=200\log{n}
. Plugging in this in
Equation

18
implies that with high probability,

y^{*}_{v}\leqslant\frac{1}{200\log n}\cdot 400\log{n}=2.

All in all, we have that for every vertex
v\in V
, with high probability

\sum_{e\ni v}y^{*}_{e}\leqslant 3.

A union bound over all the vertices, implies that
y^{*}/3
is with high probability a fractional matching. We thus have,

\Exp\left[\left|{\frac{y^{*}}{3}}\right|\mid\text{$\frac{y^{*}}{3}$ is a fractional matching, $R$}\right]\geqslant\frac{\left|{y}\right|}{3}\cdot(1-1/\mbox{\rm poly}{(n)}).

To conclude the proof of
Equation

17
, we have,

\displaystyle\Exp\left[\mu(E_{p})\mid R\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{2}{3}\cdot\Prob\left(\text{$\frac{y^{*}}{3}$ is a fractional matching}\mid R\right)\cdot\Exp\left[\left|{\frac{y^{*}}{3}}\right|\mid\text{$\frac{y^{*}}{3}$ is a fractional matching},R\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{2\left|{y}\right|}{9}\cdot(1-1/\mbox{\rm poly}(n))>\frac{\left|{y}\right|}{5};

the first inequality holds by the integrality gap of fractional matchings in
Fact

3.1
for the first term, and the trivial lower bound of
\mu(E_{p})\geqslant 0
when
y^{*}/3
is not a fractional matching;
the second inequality holds by the fact that
y^{*}/3
is with high probability a fractional matching and the expectation calculated above.

4.2.3
The Final Dynamic Streaming Algorithm: Concluding the Proof of
Theorem

1

We are almost done with the proof of
Theorem

1
. Up until now, we have already established that for any given graph
G=(V,E)
,

•

We can run
Algorithm

2
in
O(n\log^{3}{n})
space and recover the time stamps of all vertices with high probability (
Lemma

4.10
);

•

We can then extract the vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{E}
from the information collected above in an additional
O(n\log{n})
space and
O(1)
passes deterministically (
Observation

4.12
and
Observation

4.14
);

•

We can further sample the edges
E_{z}\subseteq E
in another
O(n\log^{2}{n})
space and
O(1)
passes with high probability (
Claim

4.16
);

•

Finally, we find a maximum matching in
E_{z}
; conditioned on the high probability events above, this gives us a matching
M
of
expected
size (
Lemma

4.17
):

\Exp\left|{M}\right|\geqslant\left(\frac{1-8\beta}{1-2\beta}\right)\cdot\frac{\beta}{10}\cdot\mu(G).

The main missing pieces are to
(i)
boost the probability of success of this algorithm to a high probability bound instead of an expectation-guarantee, and, subsequently,
(ii)
boost the approximation ratio of the algorithm
to a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation (and extend it even to weighted graphs). We show that both these tasks can be achieved using the existing sketching and streaming results listed in
Section

3.2
.
The proofs in the following are pretty standard.

Boosting the probability of success.

Let
G=(V,E)
be the input graph. We can assume without loss of generality that
\mu(G)\geqslant\log^{2}\!{(n)}
; the total number of edges in any graph is at most
2n\cdot\mu(G)

and thus we can run a randomized sparse-recovery in
Proposition

3.6
for recovering
2n\log^{2}\!{(n)}
edges in
O(n\log^{3}{n})
space with high probability; if
\mu(G)<\log^{2}\!{(n)}
, this algorithm succeeds in recovering the entire graph
and finding a maximum matching of
G
exactly. Otherwise, we continue with the main algorithm.

Let
\varepsilon=1/2
and run the vertex-sampling approach of
Proposition

3.7
for
O(\log{n})
guesses of
\mu(G)
as powers of
2
between
\log^{2}\!{(n)}
to
n
and run our algorithm for each guess separately in parallel.
We specify this more details in the following.

For a given guess
\tilde{\mu}
,
at the beginning of the stream, we partition the vertices randomly into groups
U_{1},\ldots,U_{t}
for
t=8\tilde{\mu}/\varepsilon=16\tilde{\mu}
. This allows us to define a graph
H:=H(\tilde{\mu})
on new vertices
\left\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{t}\right\}
(representing the groups
U_{1},\ldots,U_{t}
) and edges between
u_{i}
and
u_{j}
iff there exists at least one edge
(x,y)\in E
with
x\in U_{i}
and
y\in U_{j}
(note that multiple edges in
E
can be mapped to a single edge in
H
but
H
does
not
have multi-edges). Moreover,
an update to the dynamic stream defining
E
can be also directly translated to an update to the graph
H
and thus we can run our algorithms over
H
.
17
17

17

We should mention a minor point here.
There is a slight difference between the dynamic stream defining
E(H)
versus for
E
. In particular, we should treat the dynamic stream on
E(H)
as a vector
\phi\in\mathbb{N}^{{t}\choose{2}}
where
\phi_{u_{i},u_{j}}>0
(but not necessarily
\phi_{u_{i},u_{j}}=1
) is interpreted as the the existence of the edge
(u_{i},u_{j})\in E(H)
(whereas for the dynamic stream defining
E
, the vector
\phi\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{{V}\choose{2}}
will be the characteristic vector of
E
). Nevertheless, our algorithms work the same exact
way over
H
. This is because the only access
of these algorithms to the edges of the graph was via sparse-recovery algorithms and these algorithms return the support of the vector
\phi
(or whichever subsets of it they are being run on).
Moreover, since the entries of
\phi
are still
\mbox{\rm poly}{(n)}
bounded, the space complexity of the algorithm is exactly as before (as a function of
n
itself, i.e., we do not aim for a better dependence based on
t\leqslant n
).

Now consider, the guess
\tilde{\mu}
such that
\mu(G)\leqslant\tilde{\mu}\leqslant 2\cdot\mu(G)
. Firstly, by
Proposition

3.7
, we have that with high probability
\mu(H)\geqslant\mu(G)/2
.
As such, for this choice and by picking
\beta=1/16
in our algorithm, for the returned matching
M
, we have that

\Exp\left|{M}\right|\geqslant\left(\frac{1-8\beta}{1-2\beta}\right)\cdot\frac{\beta}{10}\cdot\mu(H)\geqslant\frac{1}{40}\cdot\mu(G).

On the other hand, in
H
, we deterministically have that
\left|{M}\right|\leqslant t/2
since it is a matching and
H
has at most
t
vertices. This means that we also always have

\left|{M}\right|\leqslant 16\mu(G).

This implies that

\Prob\left(\left|{M}\right|\geqslant\frac{1}{50}\cdot\mu(G)\right)\geqslant\frac{1}{3200}

as otherwise

\Exp\left|{M}\right|<\frac{1}{50}\cdot\mu(G)+\frac{1}{3200}\cdot 16\mu(G)<\left(\frac{1}{50}+\frac{1}{200}\right)\cdot\mu(G)=\frac{1}{40}\cdot\mu(G),

a contradiction. Repeating this algorithm now
O(\log{n})
times and returning the largest matching implies that with high probability, we will find a matching of size at least
\mu(G)/50
.
Note that given a matching in
H
, we can spend
O(1)
more passes and
O(n\log^{2}{n})
space to find a matching in
G
of the same size: for any edge
(u_{i},u_{j})
in the matching of
H
, we need
to just find a single edge between the vertices of the group
U_{i}
and
U_{j}
which we know exists. This can be done using a standard sampling trick and sparse-recovery algorithms: basically, we first count the number
c_{i,j}
of
edges between
U_{i}
and
U_{j}
in a single pass and in the second pass, we sample
\simeq\log{(n)}/c_{i,j}
fraction of vertex-pairs between
U_{i}
and
U_{j}
and run a sparse-recovery algorithm for
\simeq(\log{n})
-sparse
vectors on this subsampled pairs. With high probability, the number of sampled edges is indeed
\lesssim\log{n}
and the recovery algorithm finds at least one of those edges.

In conclusion, by running our main algorithm
O(\log^{2}{(n)})
times in parallel for different guesses of
\mu(G)
and different repetitions for each guess, we
obtain an
O(1)
-approximation algorithm to the maximum matching of any given graph
G=(V,E)
in a dynamic stream with high probability
18
18

18

To simplify the proof, we have been quite cavalier with the choice
of constants in the analysis of the algorithm and only prove a
50
-approximation guarantee. Given that we are going to boost the approximation ratio of this algorithm in a black-box way in the next step,
any constant-approximation works for us and thus we did not optimize the approximation ratio of this base algorithm. A more careful analysis of the bounds can reduce this ratio dramatically, but we suspect the limit will be
close to
6
-approximation (or possibly
4
for bipartite graphs).
. Moreover, this algorithm
uses
O(n\log^{5}{n})
space and
O(\log\log{n})
passes.

Boosting the approximation ratio.

Now that we have an
O(1)
-approximation algorithm that succeeds with high probability, this step becomes a black-box application of
Proposition

3.8
.

This concludes the proof of
Theorem

1
.

5
The Lower Bound

We provide our multi-pass lower bound for approximate matchings in the dynamic streaming model in this section, formalizing
Result

2
.

Theorem 3
.

Any semi-streaming algorithm that given any
n
-vertex graph in a dynamic streams, outputs an
O(1)
-approximation to the maximum matching problem with any constant probability of success requires
\Omega(\log\log n)
passes.

We prove
Theorem

3
using the connection between the streaming and communication models (
Proposition

3.10
). Our proof consists of two separate parts proven in the subsequent sections.

Part 1.
In
Section

5.1
, we define a new two-party communication problem for
r
-round protocols called Augmented Hidden Matrices (
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
, see
Definition

5.3
) and prove the following lower bound on its communication complexity in
Section

5.1.1
.

Lemma 5.1
.

For any sufficiently large
n_{r}\in\mathbb{N}
, positive integer
r=O(\log\log\!{(n_{r})})
, positive number
\alpha\leqslant 1-n_{r}^{-1/2r}
and constant
c_{\textsc{adv}}\geqslant 1
, any
r
-round protocol that successfully solves
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
with
probability of success at least

\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{r}{20\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)}\right)

must have a communication cost of at least

s_{r}=\left(n_{r}\right)\uparrow\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{r\cdot 2^{r}}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\frac{(\alpha/(1-\alpha))^{2}}{(c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2})^{r}\cdot((r+1)!)^{3}}.

Part 2.
In
Section

5.2
, we construct graph instances corresponding to the
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
problem. We prove the following connection between any
p
-pass dynamic streaming algorithm for approximate maximum matching and
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
where
r=2p-1
in
Section

5.2.3
.

Lemma 5.2
.

For any sufficiently large
n_{r}\in\mathbb{N}
, integers
p,s\geqslant 1
, and number
\beta\geqslant 1
, let
\mathcal{A}
by any
p
-pass
s
-space
dynamic streaming algorithm that computes a
\beta
-approximate maximum matching on a
(4n_{r})
-vertex bipartite graphs with probability of success at least
1-1/\mbox{\rm poly}{(n)}
.
Then, for
r=2p-1
and
\alpha=1/(4\cdot\beta\cdot r)
, there exists an
r
-round protocol
\pi
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
with
probability of success

\textnormal{suc}(\pi)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{3\beta}\right)

and
communication cost

\textsc{CC}(\pi)\leqslant r\cdot s+O(r\cdot n_{r}\log{(n_{r})}).

We now prove
Theorem

3
using the above two lemmas.

Proof of
Theorem

3
.

Without loss of generality, we can solely focus on proving the lower bound for semi-streaming algorithms that succeed with high probability instead of constant probability.
This is due to the following standard reduction.

Simply run a constant probability of success streaming algorithm in parallel
O(\log{n})
times
and consider all the outputs; then, spend one more pass to filter out all the ones that output an edge that does not belong to the graph (because they failed), and among the rest, output the largest matching.
This way, we still obtain a semi-streaming algorithm with just one more pass (which is negligible in this context) and a high probability of success. As such, in the rest of the proof, we focus on high-probability-of-success algorithms.

For sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}
, integers
p,s\geqslant 1
and any constant
\beta\geqslant 1
,
let
\mathcal{A}
be any
p
-pass
s
-space dynamic streaming algorithm that computes a
\beta
-approximate maximum matching on any
(4n)
-vertex graph with probability of success at least
1-1/\mbox{\rm poly}{(n)}
.

By
Lemma

5.2
with
r=2p-1
and
\alpha=1/(4\cdot\beta\cdot r)
, there exists an
r
-round protocol
\pi
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n,\alpha)
where

\textnormal{suc}(\pi)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{3\beta}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\textsc{CC}(\pi)\leqslant r\cdot s+O(r^{2}\cdot n\log n).

By
Lemma

5.1
with
n_{r}=n
and
c_{\textsc{adv}}=\beta
, we have that
\textsc{CC}(\pi)\geqslant s_{r}
since

\textnormal{suc}(\pi)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{3\beta}\right)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{r}{20\cdot\beta\cdot(r+1)}\right).

Then, by combining the bounds on
\textsc{CC}(\pi)
and re-arranging the inequality, we obtain the following lower bound on the space of
\mathcal{A}
using the fact that
c_{\textsc{jpy}}
and
\beta
are constants (and thus
\alpha=\Theta(1/r)
):

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{s_{r}}{r}-O(n\log n)

\displaystyle={\left(n\right)\uparrow\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{r\cdot 2^{r}}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\frac{(\alpha/(1-\alpha))^{2}}{(c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot\beta^{2}\cdot 80^{2})^{r}\cdot((r+1)!)^{3}}}\cdot\frac{1}{r}-O(n\log n)

\displaystyle\geqslant{\left(n\right)\uparrow\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{r}-1}\right)}\cdot r^{-\Theta(r)}-O(n\log n).

(19)

Overall, by considering
\mathcal{A}
with
p=o(\log\log n)
and thus
r=2p-1=o(\log\log n)
, we have that the space used by the algorithm is
s\gg n\cdot\mbox{\rm polylog}(n)
using
Equation

19
.
Therefore, any dynamic streaming algorithm that uses
n\cdot{\mbox{\rm polylog}}(n)
space, namely, a semi-streaming algorithm, to compute an
O(1)
-approximate maximum matching must use
\Omega(\log\log n)
passes.

5.1
Augmented Hidden Matrices

For any
r\geqslant 1
, the
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
problem is defined recursively using
b_{r}^{2}
many instances of the
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
problem where
n_{r},b_{r},n_{r-1}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
The output of any instance is a single bit, pointed to by a
search sequence
(to be defined soon).

Definition 5.3

(Augmented Hidden Matrices)
.

The Augmented Hidden Matrices problem, denoted by
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
, is defined as follows (see
Figure

9
for an illustration):

•

For
\mathbf{r=0}
.
Alice does not receive any input, i.e.,
A^{(0)}=\emptyset
, and Bob receives a single
bit
B^{(0)}\in\{0,1\}
