. There is no communication and Alice has to output
B^{(0)}
.

•

For
\mathbf{r\geqslant 1}
.
Start with
b_{r}^{2}
instances of the players’ inputs in
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
, denoted by
(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
for
i,j\in[b_{r}]
.
Alice receives
A^{(r)}=X
where
X
is a
b_{r}\times b_{r}
matrix such that
X[i,j]=B^{(r-1)}_{i,j}
for
i,j\in[b_{r}]
.
Bob receives the input
B^{(r)}=(\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}},Y)
where
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}\in S_{b_{r}}
are permutations of
[b_{r}]
and
Y
is a
b_{r}\times b_{r}
matrix such that, for
i,j\in[b_{r}]
,

\displaystyle Y[i,j]=\begin{cases}\left(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j}\right)&\text{if }b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\neq\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r},\\
A^{(r-1)}_{i,j}&\text{if }b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)=\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r},\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Alice starts the communication. After all the messages are sent,
the player receiving the last message is given a
search sequence
, which is a tuple of
r
integers,
(k^{\star}_{r},k^{\star}_{r-1},\ldots,k^{\star}_{1})
with
k^{\star}_{i}\in[b_{i}\cdot(1-\alpha)]
. This player must output the solution to
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
instance
(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
where
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)=\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)=b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k^{\star}_{r}
using the search sequence
(k^{\star}_{r-1},k^{\star}_{r-2}\ldots k^{\star}_{1})
.

We expound more on the defintion of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
problem, and give some useful terminology next.

For
r=0
case, we set
n_{0}=1
, and
\mathsf{AHM}_{0}(1,\alpha)
is also denoted by
\mathsf{AHM}_{0}(1)
. This is a base case that is trivially hard for
0
-round protocols.

For each of the
b_{r}^{2}
many
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
instances
(A^{(r-1)},B^{(r-1)})
used in an instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
, the roles of Alice and Bob are swapped, i.e., Alice holds
B^{(r-1)}
and Bob holds
A^{(r-1)}
.
Out of these instances, we call the set of the
\bm{k_{r}}:=b_{r}\cdot(1-\alpha)
many
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
instances
(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
where
b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)=\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}
as the
special sub-instances
in an instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
.
Since each special sub-instance identifies another set of
k_{r-1}
many
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-2}
special (sub-)sub-instances, recursively applying this for all special sub-instances of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
identifies a set of
k_{r}\cdot k_{r-1}\cdot\ldots\cdot k_{1}
many
\mathsf{AHM}_{0}

special base instances
, where each one corresponds to a single bit
B^{(0)}
.

It is useful to point to the number of special base instances explicitly for later.

Observation 5.4
.

In any instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
, there are
k_{r}\cdot k_{r-1}\cdot\ldots\cdot k_{1}
many special base instances where
k_{i}=b_{i}\cdot(1-\alpha)
for each
i\in[r]
.

Furthermore, we call the set of
k_{r}^{2}-k_{r}
many
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
instances
(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
where
b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\neq\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}
as the
off-diagonal sub-instances
in an instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
. We define some more notation to point to the sub-instances.

Notation.

In any instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
, denoted by
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
, we use the following notation.
We denote each of the
k_{r}
many special sub-instances as
(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k})=(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
where
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)=\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)=b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k
and
i,j\in[b_{r}]
for
k\in[k_{r}]
.
We collectively denote these special sub-instances as
(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}})
where

A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}=(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{1},A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{2},\ldots,A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k_{r}})\quad\text{and}\quad B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}=(B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{1},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{2},\ldots,B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k_{r}}).

We denote the collection of the
k_{r}^{2}-k_{r}
many off-diagonal sub-instances as
(A^{\textnormal{{off}}},B^{\textnormal{{off}}})
where

A^{\textnormal{{off}}}=(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j}:b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\neq\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r})\quad\text{and}\quad B^{\textnormal{{off}}}=(B^{(r-1)}_{i,j}:b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\neq\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}).

We let
B^{\textnormal{{rest}}}
be the remaining
b_{r}^{2}-k_{r}^{2}
sub-instances
B^{(r-1)}_{i,j}
where
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\leqslant b_{r}\cdot\alpha
or
\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}\cdot\alpha
.

Search Sequences.

After all the messages are sent, the search sequence given to the player who receives the last message is always
uniformly random
and
independent of the players’ inputs
. That is, a search sequence
(k^{\star}_{r},k^{\star}_{r-1},\ldots,k^{\star}_{1})
is chosen where for
i\in[r]
,
k^{\star}_{i}
is chosen uniformly at random and independently from
[b_{i}\cdot(1-\alpha)]
.
The solution to any instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
given
(k^{\star}_{r},k^{\star}_{r-1},\dots,k^{\star}_{1})
is the same as the solution to the uniformly chosen special sub-instance
(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k^{\star}_{r}},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k^{\star}_{r}})
on the search sequence
(k^{\star}_{r-1},\dots,k^{\star}_{1})
.
Continuing this until the last index
k^{\star}_{1}
in the search sequence ultimately identifies a uniform random special base instance, i.e., an instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{0}
, whose solution is the solution to the
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
instance.

Our definition of the
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
communication problem is non-standard as the uniformly random search sequence, which is a part of the input, is given to the final player
at the very end of a protocol
.
In particular, the search sequence is crucial to defining the output of the problem, but does not form part of the input held by the players throughout the protocol.

Although the search sequence defines the communication problem in a non-standard way, the easy direction of Yao’s minmax theorem is still applicable.
Thus,
we prove
Lemma

5.1
by considering deterministic protocols for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
where the players’ inputs
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
are sampled from the hard distribution (which we define shortly).

Remark 2
.

The definition of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
(
Definition

5.3
) always includes the uniform random search sequence, which is independent of the players’ inputs and how they are distributed.
However, when referring to the
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
problem, we do not explicitly mention the given search sequence if this is clear from context.

\vdots
\dots

Figure 9
:

An illustration of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
where
b_{r}=7
,
k^{\star}_{r}=2
, and
\alpha=2/7
, i.e.,
b_{r}\cdot\alpha=2
.
In this figure, the
b_{r}\times b_{r}
matrix is presented after applying the respective permutations of rows and columns using
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}
and
\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}
, which are held by Bob (alternatively, think of
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}
and
\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}
as identity permutations here).
In each position of the matrix, the corresponding input is either a complete instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
denoted by
(A,B)=(A^{(r-1)},B^{(r-1)})
or a partial instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
denoted by
B=B^{(r-1)}
due to the asymmetry of the construction.
All of the
B
’s are held by Alice, all of the
A
’s are held by Bob, and Bob further holds the
B
’s corresponding to
B^{\textnormal{{off}}}
, which are bolded and underlined.
The shaded instances are the special sub-instances
(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}})
, i.e., the ones in the diagonal of the bolded box.
The remaining
B
’s belong to
B^{\textnormal{{rest}}}
, i.e., the ones not in the bolded box.
Given the search sequence
(k^{\star}_{r},k^{\star}_{r-1},\ldots,k^{\star}_{1})
, the goal is to solve the the instance identified by
{k^{\star}_{r}}=2
(shaded in green, and denoted by
(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{2},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{2})
), using the search sequence
(k^{\star}_{r-1},\ldots,k^{\star}_{1})
.

Next, we fix the parameters of the problem, and then define our hard distribution
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
.

Parameter Choices.

For all
r\geqslant 1
, it is sufficient for us to consider the

\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
problem for parameters that satisfy the following conditions:

\begin{split}n_{r}&=n_{r-1}\cdot b_{r}\\
b_{r}&=\left(n_{r}\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{2^{r-1}}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{r\cdot 2^{r-1}}{2^{r}-1}-1\right)\\
n_{r-1}&=\left(n_{r}\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{2^{r-1}-1}{2^{r}-1}\right)/\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{r\cdot 2^{r-1}}{2^{r}-1}-1\right).\end{split}

(20)

From now on, we only consider
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
with these parameter choices.

Hard Distribution
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
:

•

For
\mathbf{r=0}
.

Sample
B^{(0)}\in\{0,1\}
uniformly and independently. We also use
\mathcal{D}_{0}(1)
to denote this distribution.

•

For
\mathbf{r\geqslant 1}
.

Sample the
b_{r}^{2}
instances
(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
from
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
independently for each
i,j\in[b_{r}]
where
b_{r},n_{r-1}
are as defined in
Equation

20
. Sample the permutations
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}\in S_{b_{r}}
uniformly and independently.

Recall that the distribution of the search sequence was already defined to be uniformly random, and independent of all other inputs.

We have the following obvious observation.

Observation 5.5
.

Any
0
-round protocol (wherein Alice outputs the answer) for solving
\mathsf{AHM}_{0}(1)
succeeds with probability at most
1/2
when the input is sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{0}(1)
.

The proof of
Lemma

5.1
relies on a round-elimination argument that shows that if there is a good deterministic protocol for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
under the input distribution
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
, then there must be a good deterministic protocol for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
under the input distribution
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
.
We prove the following key lemma in
Section

5.1.2
.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any
r\geqslant 1
, sufficiently large
n_{r}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha,\delta\in(0,1)
, and integer
s\geqslant 1
,
suppose there exists a deterministic
r
-round protocol
\pi_{r}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
with

\textsc{CC}(\pi_{r})\leqslant s

and probability of success at least
\delta
when the input is sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
.
Then, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists a deterministic
(r-1)
-round protocol
\pi_{r-1}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
with

\textsc{CC}(\pi_{r-1})\leqslant c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot(r/\varepsilon)\cdot\left(\frac{s}{k_{r}}+r\right),

and probability of success

\textnormal{suc}(\pi_{r-1})\geqslant\delta-\varepsilon-\sqrt{\frac{s}{2\cdot b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}}

when the players’ input is sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
.

With that, we have the main tools required to prove of our lower bound for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
.

5.1.1
The Lower Bound for Augmented Hidden Matrices: Proof of
Lemma

5.1

Proof of
Lemma

5.1
.

We prove this result by first assuming the existence of a deterministic
r
-round protocol
\pi_{r}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
that is ‘too good to be true’ (w.r.t. the statement of the lemma) on inputs sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
. Then, by iteratively applying the round-elimination argument in
Lemma

5.6
, we ultimately obtain a deterministic
0
-round protocol
\pi_{0}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{0}
that is trivially ‘too good to be true’ on inputs sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{0}(1)
(given
Observation

5.5
), a contradiction.
Formally, we prove this by induction on
r
.

Base case for
\mathbf{r=1}
.

Suppose that there exists a deterministic
1
-round protocol
\pi_{1}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{1}(n_{1},\alpha)
with communication cost

\textsc{CC}(\pi_{1})<s_{1}=\frac{n_{1}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}{c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2}\cdot 8}

and probability of success

\textnormal{suc}(\pi_{1})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{40\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}}\right)

when the input is sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{1}(n_{1},\alpha)
.
Then, using
Lemma

5.6
with
\varepsilon=1/(160\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}})
, we obtain a deterministic
0
-round protocol
\pi_{0}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{0}(1)
which has no communication cost (since no messages are communicated) and succeeds with probability

\displaystyle\textnormal{suc}(\pi_{0})

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{40\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}}\right)-\frac{1}{160\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}}-\sqrt{\frac{n_{1}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}{c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2}\cdot 16\cdot b_{1}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{80\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}}-\sqrt{\frac{n_{1}^{2}}{c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 160^{2}\cdot b_{1}^{2}}}\right)

(since
c_{\textsc{jpy}}\geqslant 1
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{160\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}}\right)

(since
b_{1}=n_{1}
by
Equation

20
)

when the input is sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{0}(1)
.
This, however, contradicts
Observation

5.5
and thus proves the result for the base case when
r=1
.

Inductive step for
\mathbf{r\geqslant 2}
.
Having proven the base case, we now prove the result for any
r\geqslant 2
given the result holds for
r-1
, i.e., the inductive hypothesis. Suppose that there exists a deterministic protocol
\pi_{r}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
with communication cost

\textsc{CC}(\pi_{r})<s_{r}=\left(n_{r}\right)\uparrow\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{r\cdot 2^{r}}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\frac{(\alpha/(1-\alpha))^{2}}{(c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2})^{r}\cdot((r+1)!)^{3}}

and probability of success

\textnormal{suc}(\pi_{r})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{r}{20\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)}\right)

when the input is sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
.
Then, using
Lemma

5.6
with

\varepsilon=1/{(40\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)^{2})}
,
we obtain a deterministic
(r-1)
-round protocol
\pi_{r-1}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
with cost

\displaystyle\textsc{CC}(\pi_{r-1})<c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot(40\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)^{2}\cdot r)\cdot\left(s_{r}/k_{r}+r\right)

(21)

and probability of success

\displaystyle\textnormal{suc}(\pi_{r-1})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{r}{20\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)}\right)-\frac{1}{40\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)^{2}}-\sqrt{\frac{s_{r}}{2\cdot b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}}

(22)

when the input is sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
.

We now argue that protocol
\pi_{r-1}
contradicts the result for
r-1
, namely, the inductive hypothesis.
In particular, we show that
\textsc{CC}(\pi_{r-1})<s_{r-1}
and

\textnormal{suc}(\pi_{r-1})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{(r-1)}{20\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot r}\right).

The rest of the proof is a careful but rather tedious calculation of the values we obtain using the above reduction for the communication cost and probability of success of the protocol.

To bound the communication cost, we first reiterate the relation between
n_{r}
and
n_{r-1}
from
Equation

20
as follows:

\displaystyle\left(n_{r}\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{1}{2^{r}-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(n_{r-1}\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{1}{2^{r-1}-1}\right)\cdot\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(\left(\frac{r\cdot 2^{r-1}}{2^{r}-1}-1\right)\cdot\frac{1}{2^{r-1}-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(n_{r-1}\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{1}{2^{r-1}-1}\right)\cdot\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{(r-2)\cdot 2^{r-1}+1}{(2^{r}-1)\cdot(2^{r-1}-1)}\right).

(23)

Next, we use this to simplify the expression for the term
s_{r}/k_{r}
in
Equation

21
.

\displaystyle\frac{s_{r}}{k_{r}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(n_{r}\right)\uparrow\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{r\cdot 2^{r}}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\frac{(\alpha/(1-\alpha))^{2}}{(c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2})^{r}\cdot((r+1)!)^{3}}}_{s_{r}}\cdot\frac{1}{\underbrace{b_{r}\cdot(1-\alpha)}_{1/k_{r}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{n_{r}}{b_{r}}\cdot\left(n_{r}\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{1}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{r\cdot 2^{r}}{2^{r}-1}-1\right)\cdot\frac{(\alpha/(1-\alpha))^{2}}{(c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2})^{r}\cdot((r+1)!)^{3}}

\displaystyle=n_{r-1}\cdot\left(n_{r}\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{1}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{r\cdot 2^{r}}{2^{r}-1}-1\right)\cdot\frac{(\alpha/(1-\alpha))^{2}}{(c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2})^{r}\cdot((r+1)!)^{3}}

(as
n_{r}=n_{r-1}\cdot b_{r}
by
Equation

20
)

\displaystyle=\left(n_{r-1}\right)\uparrow\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{r-1}-1}\right)\cdot\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(x\right)\cdot\frac{(\alpha/(1-\alpha))^{2}}{(c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2})^{r}\cdot((r+1)!)^{3}}.

(by
Equation

23
)

where the exponent of
(1-\alpha)
in the above equation is

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=\frac{(r-2)\cdot 2^{r-1}+1}{(2^{r}-1)\cdot(2^{r-1}-1)}+\frac{r\cdot 2^{r}}{2^{r}-1}-1

\displaystyle=\frac{(r-2)\cdot(2^{r-1}-1)+1+r-2}{(2^{r}-1)\cdot(2^{r-1}-1)}+\frac{(r-1)\cdot 2^{r}+1}{2^{r}-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{r}-1}\cdot\left((r-1)2^{r}+1+(r-2)+\frac{r-1}{2^{r-1}-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(r-1)}{2^{r}-1}\cdot(2^{r}+1+\frac{1}{2^{r-1}-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{(r-1)}{2^{r}-1}\cdot(\frac{2^{2r-1}-2^{r}+2^{r-1}-1+1}{2^{r-1}-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{(r-1)}{2^{r}-1}\cdot(\frac{2^{2r-1}-2^{r-1}}{2^{r-1}-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{(r-1)\cdot 2^{r-1}}{2^{r-1}-1}\ .

Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{s_{r}}{k_{r}}

\displaystyle=\left(n_{r-1}\right)\uparrow\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{r-1}-1}\right)\cdot\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{(r-1)\cdot 2^{r-1}}{2^{r-1}-1}\right)\cdot\frac{(\alpha/(1-\alpha))^{2}}{(c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2})^{r}\cdot((r+1)!)^{3}}

\displaystyle=s_{r-1}\cdot\frac{1}{c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2}\cdot(r+1)^{3}}.

By plugging this back into
Equation

21
, we obtain

\displaystyle\textsc{CC}(\pi_{r-1})

\displaystyle<c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot(40\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)^{2}\cdot r)\cdot\left(s_{r-1}\cdot\frac{1}{c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2}\cdot(r+1)^{3}}+r\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 160}\cdot s_{r-1}+40\cdot c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)^{4}

(using
r\leqslant r+1
)

\displaystyle\leqslant s_{r-1}\ ,

(as
r^{4}\ll s_{r-1}
for
r=O(\log\log n)
and
\alpha\leqslant 1-n_{r}^{-1/2r}
)

which is our desired bound on the communication cost of
\pi_{r-1}
.

To bound the probability of success, we first reiterate the following useful relation from
Equation

20
:

\displaystyle b_{r}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(n_{r}\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{2\cdot 2^{r-1}}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{r\cdot 2\cdot 2^{r-1}}{2^{r}-1}-2\right)

\displaystyle=\left(n_{r}\right)\uparrow\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{r\cdot 2^{r}}{2^{r}-1}\right)/(1-\alpha)^{2}\ .

(24)

Using this, we now simplify the term
s_{r}/(2\cdot b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2})
in
Equation

22
.

\displaystyle\frac{s_{r}}{2\cdot b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(n_{r}\right)\uparrow\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\left(1-\alpha\right)\uparrow\left(\frac{r\cdot 2^{r}}{2^{r}-1}\right)\cdot\frac{(\alpha/(1-\alpha))^{2}}{(c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2})^{r}\cdot((r+1)!)^{3}}}_{s_{r}}\cdot\frac{1}{2\cdot b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}{(c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2})^{r}\cdot((r+1)!)^{3}}\cdot\frac{1}{2\cdot b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}

(by
Equation

24
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\cdot(c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2})^{r}\cdot((r+1)!)^{3}}\ .

By plugging this back into
Equation

22
, we have that

\displaystyle\textnormal{suc}(\pi_{r-1})

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{r}{20\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)}\right)-\frac{1}{40\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)^{2}}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\cdot(c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2})^{r}\cdot((r+1)!)^{3}}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{r}{20\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)}\right)-\frac{1}{40\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)^{2}}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{c_{\textsc{adv}}^{2}\cdot 80^{2}\cdot(r+1)^{4}}}

(since
c_{\textsc{jpy}}\geqslant 1
and
2\cdot((r+1)!)^{3}\geqslant(r+1)^{4}
for
r\geqslant 1
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{r}{20\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)}\right)-\frac{1}{c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot 80\cdot(r+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{r}{20\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)}-\frac{1}{40\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot(r+1)^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{r-1}{20\cdot c_{\textsc{adv}}\cdot r}\right),

which is our desired bound.

Overall, we have that the deterministic protocol
\pi_{r-1}
constructed from
\pi_{r}
using
Lemma

5.6
contradicts the lemma for
r-1
(inductive hypothesis) and thus the lemma must hold for deterministic
r
-round protocols when the input is sampled from the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{r}
for every
r\geqslant 1
.
Finally, by the easy direction of Yao’s minmax principle, the same result holds for any (even randomized)
r
-round protocol for any instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
(given a uniform random search sequence).

5.1.2
The Round Elimination Argument: Proof of
Lemma

5.6

We now get to the main part of the argument, which is the proof of
Lemma

5.6
, restated below.

Lemma

5.6
(restated).

For any
r\geqslant 1
, sufficiently large
n_{r}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha,\delta\in(0,1)
, and integer
s\geqslant 1
,
suppose there exists a deterministic
r
-round protocol
\pi_{r}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
with

\textsc{CC}(\pi_{r})\leqslant s

and probability of success at least
\delta
when the input is sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
.
Then, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists a deterministic
(r-1)
-round protocol
\pi_{r-1}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
with

\textsc{CC}(\pi_{r-1})\leqslant c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot(r/\varepsilon)\cdot\left(\frac{s}{k_{r}}+r\right),

and probability of success

\textnormal{suc}(\pi_{r-1})\geqslant\delta-\varepsilon-\sqrt{\frac{s}{2\cdot b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}}

when the players’ input is sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
.

Starting with a deterministic
r
-round protocol
\pi_{r}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
where
\textsc{CC}(\pi_{r})\leqslant s
and
\textnormal{suc}(\pi_{r})\geqslant\delta
under
\mathcal{D}_{r}
as in the statement of
Lemma

5.6
, we construct a deterministic
(r-1)
-round protocol
\pi_{r-1}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
that proves the lemma in three steps:

(i)

(Input Embedding)
Construct a randomized
r
-round protocol
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
under
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}
with success probability the same as
\pi_{r}
under
\mathcal{D}_{r}
, i.e.,
\textnormal{suc}(\pi^{(1)}_{r})\geqslant\delta
, and internal information cost less than the communication cost of
\pi_{r}
by a
k_{r}
multiplicative factor, i.e.,

\textsc{IC}(\pi^{(1)}_{r},\mathcal{D}_{r-1})\leqslant\frac{s}{k_{r}}.

(ii)

(Message Compression)
Construct a randomized
r
-round protocol
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
under
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}
with success probability less than that of
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
under
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}
by at most an additive
\varepsilon
factor, i.e.,
\textnormal{suc}(\pi^{(2)}_{r})\geqslant\delta-\varepsilon
, and with communication cost similar to the internal information cost of
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
under
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}
, i.e.,

\textsc{CC}(\pi^{(2)}_{r})\leqslant c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot\frac{r}{\varepsilon}\cdot\left(\textsc{IC}(\pi^{(1)}_{r},\mathcal{D}_{r-1})+r\right)\leqslant c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot\frac{r}{\varepsilon}\cdot\left(\frac{s}{k_{r}}+r\right).

(iii)

(Guessing the First Message)
Construct a deterministic
(r-1)
-round protocol
\pi_{r-1}
under
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}
with success probability less than that of
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
under
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}
by at most an additive
\simeq\sqrt{s/(b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2})}
factor, i.e,
\textnormal{suc}(\pi_{r-1})\geqslant\delta-\varepsilon-\sqrt{s/(2\cdot b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2})}
, and with communication cost no more than that of
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
, i.e.,

\textsc{CC}(\pi_{r-1})\leqslant\textsc{CC}(\pi^{(2)}_{r})\leqslant c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot\frac{r}{\varepsilon}\cdot\left(\frac{s}{k_{r}}+r\right).

Step

(
i
)

: A Low Information Cost Protocol via Input Embedding

We construct a low information cost
r
-round protocol
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
by considering an input
(A^{(r-1)},B^{(r-1)})\sim\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
and embedding it in a simulated input instance
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})\sim\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
such that its output can be used to solve
(A^{(r-1)},B^{(r-1)})
.
Then, the players simulate a run of the protocol
\pi_{r}
in
r
rounds using the simulated input.

To simplify the exposition, we disambiguate the players in the construction of
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
(and
\pi_{r-1}
later in step

(

⁢
i
i
i

)

).
We use Alice and Bob to denote the players in the input instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
where Alice holds
A^{\star}=A^{(r-1)}
and Bob holds
B^{\star}=B^{(r-1)}
.
On the other hand, we use
{P}_{X}
and
{P}_{Y}
to refer to the players in the simulated input instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
where
player
{P}_{X}
holds
A^{(r)}=X
and player
{P}_{Y}
holds
B^{(r)}=(\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}},Y)
.

An
r
-round protocol
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
on input
(A^{\star},B^{\star})\sim\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
where
(k^{\star}_{r-1},k^{\star}_{r-2},\dots,k^{\star}_{1})
is the given uniform random search sequence
:

(a)

Using
public randomness
, independently sample a uniform random search index
k^{\star}\in[k_{r}]
. Then, jointly sample the following from
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
:

•

The uniform random permutations
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}
of
[b_{r}]
;

•

Bob’s part of all the
k_{r}^{2}-k_{r}
many off-diagonal sub-instances

B^{\textnormal{{off}}}=(B^{(r-1)}_{i,j}:b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\neq\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r});

•

Alice’s part of
k^{\star}-1
many special sub-instances
A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k^{\star}}=(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}:1\leqslant k<k^{\star})
;

•

Bob’s part of
k_{r}-k^{\star}
many special sub-instances
B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>k^{\star}}=(B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}:k^{\star}<k\leqslant k_{r})
.

Let
i^{\star},j^{\star}\in[b_{r}]
be such that
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)=\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)=b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k^{\star}
, which both players can compute since
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}},k^{\star}
are sampled using public randomness.

(b)

Bob takes on the role of player
{P}_{X}
and sets
X[i^{\star},j^{\star}]=B^{\star}
.
Bob
privately samples

X
from
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
conditioned on all the random variables from step

(
a
)

and
X[i^{\star},j^{\star}]
, i.e., the remainder of
{P}_{X}
’s input
A^{(r)}
.

(c)

Alice takes on the role of player
{P}_{Y}
and sets
Y[i^{\star},j^{\star}]=A^{\star}
.
Alice
privately samples

Y
from
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
conditioned on all the variables from step

(
a
)

and
Y[i^{\star},j^{\star}]
, i.e., the remainder of player
{P}_{Y}
’s input
B^{(r)}
.

(d)

The players
{P}_{X}
(
=\text{Bob}
) and
{P}_{Y}
(
=\text{Alice}
) simulate a run of protocol
\pi_{r}
in
r
rounds using their respective inputs
A^{(r)}=X
and
B^{(r)}=(\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}},Y)
and starting with player
{P}_{X}
.

(e)

At the end of the protocol, the player that receives the final message gets the uniform random search sequence
(k^{\star}_{r-1},k^{\star}_{r-2},\ldots,k^{\star}_{0})
and then returns the answer of
\pi_{r}
on the search sequence
(k^{\star},k^{\star}_{r-1},k^{\star}_{r-2},\ldots,k^{\star}_{0})
.

Observation 5.7
.

In protocol
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
,
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
in the simulation of
\pi_{r}
is sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
and
(k^{\star},k^{\star}_{r-1},k^{\star}_{r-2},\ldots,k^{\star}_{0})
is a uniform random search sequence.

Proof.

The input to the simulation of
\pi_{r}
is
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
where
A^{(r)}=X
and
B^{(r)}=(\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}},Y)
.
All variables of the input are jointly sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
except for Alice and Bob’s embedded instance
(A^{\star},B^{\star})\sim\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
where
X[i^{\star},j^{\star}]=B^{\star}
and
Y[i^{\star},j^{\star}]=A^{\star}
.
Since the instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
corresponding to
(X[i^{\star},j^{\star}],Y[i^{\star},j^{\star}])
is independent of all other variables in the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
, all other variables are jointly distributed according to
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
.
It remains to show that
(X[i^{\star},j^{\star}],Y[i^{\star},j^{\star}])
distributed according to
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
, which is required in
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
.
This is immediate from the embedding as
(X[i^{\star},j^{\star}],Y[i^{\star},j^{\star}])=(A^{\star},B^{\star})\sim\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)

Each search index in
(k^{\star}_{r-1},k^{\star}_{r-2},\ldots,k^{\star}_{0})
is uniformly sampled. Then, since
k^{\star}\in[k_{r}]
is also uniformly sampled, we have
(k^{\star},k^{\star}_{r-1},k^{\star}_{r-2},\ldots,k^{\star}_{0})
is a random search sequence as required.

With this observation, we have that Alice and Bob have successfully simulated
\pi_{r}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
on the correct distribution, as the search sequence
(k^{\star},k^{\star}_{r-1},k^{\star}_{r-2},\ldots,k^{\star}_{0})
is uniformly random and the players’ inputs are distributed according to
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
.
Since the messages communicated by the players are exactly the messages of the simulated protocol
\pi_{r}
, the communication cost of
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
is the same as
\pi_{r}
. Although the communication cost is large, we now show that its internal information cost (about input
(A^{\star},B^{\star})
) is smaller by a multiplicative
k_{r}
factor.

Notation.

We use
{\mathsf{A}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\star}
to denote the random variables corresponding to the input instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
given to Alice and Bob, which are distributed according to
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}
. We use
{\mathsf{\Pi}}
to denote the random variable corresponding to the set of messages sent by both Alice and Bob. For
i\in[r]
,
\Pi_{i}
denotes the message sent in round
i
and
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{i}
denotes the random variable corresponding to
\Pi_{i}
.
We use
{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
to denote the random variables corresponding to
A^{\textnormal{{off}}},B^{\textnormal{{off}}}
,
k^{\star},A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}=(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{1},A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{2},\ldots,A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k_{r}})
,
B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}=(B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{1},\ldots,B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k_{r}})
, respectively.

The following claim proves that information cost of
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
about its input sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}
is
1/k_{r}
times smaller than the information cost of
\pi_{r}
about its own input sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r}
.

Claim 5.8
.

In protocol
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
, we have that

\textsc{IC}(\pi^{(1)}_{r},\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha))\leqslant\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\textsc{IC}(\pi_{r},\mathcal{D}_{r}(n,\alpha)).

Proof.

By the definition of internal information cost (
Definition

3.11
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\textsc{IC}(\pi^{(1)}_{r},\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha))&=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\star}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\underbrace{\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}}_{\text{public randomness of $\pi^{(1)}_{r}$}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\star})\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\star}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\underbrace{\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}}_{\text{public randomness $\pi^{(1)}_{r}$}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\star}).\end{split}

(25)

Each mutual information term corresponds to the amount of information communicated by each player about their input, and we bound them separately.

For Alice’s mutual information term in
Equation

25
, we have that

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\star}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\star})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\sum_{k\in[k_{r}]}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\star}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}=k,{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>k},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k},{\mathsf{B}}^{\star})

(by the definition of conditional mutual information and the uniform distribution of
{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\sum_{k\in[k_{r}]}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}=k,{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\geqslant k},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k})

(as
{\mathsf{A}}^{\star}={\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k},{\mathsf{B}}^{\star}={\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}
in protocol
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
when
{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}=k
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\sum_{k\in[k_{r}]}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\geqslant k},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k});

the last part holds because the joint distribution of
({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\leqslant k},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\geqslant k},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},\Pi)
is independent of the value of
{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}
.
In particular, if we sample any input
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
from
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n,\alpha)
(which does not include
k^{\star}=k^{\star}_{r}
), the values of
\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\leqslant k},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\geqslant k}
are fixed and the value of
{\mathsf{\Pi}}
is also fixed as
\pi_{r}
is deterministic. Hence, the distribution of
{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}
remains uniform over
[k_{r}]
irrespective of the instance
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
.
Continuing the bounding of Alice’s mutual information term, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\sum_{k\in[k_{r}]}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\geqslant k},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k})

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt\leqslant\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\sum_{k\in[k_{r}]}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k})

(by
Proposition

A.2
as
{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k}\perp{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k}

by the definition of distribution
\mathcal{D}_{r}
)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt=\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}})

(by the chain rule of mutual information,
Fact

A.1
-(
4
))

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt\leqslant\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}})

(by
Proposition

A.2
, as
{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}\perp{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}}\mid{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}}
by
the definition of distribution
\mathcal{D}_{r}
)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt=\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}})

(as
{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}}
is fixed)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt\leqslant\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}})

(by the chain rule (
Fact

A.1
-(
4
)) and non-negativity of mutual information (
Fact

A.1
-(
2
)))

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt=\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{(r)}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid{\mathsf{A}}^{(r)}).

(recall that input of
{P}_{Y}
(resp.
{P}_{X}
) simulated by Alice (resp. Bob) in
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
is
B^{(r)}
(resp.
A^{(r)}
))

For Bob’s mutual information term in
Equation

25
, following a similar argument, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\star}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\star})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\sum_{k\in[k_{r}]}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}=k,{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>k},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\leqslant k})

(by definition of conditional mutual information and
{\mathsf{A}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\star}
and the uniform distribution of
{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\sum_{k\in[k_{r}]}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>k},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\leqslant k})

(as in the previous case for Alice, for any
k\in[k_{r}]
,
({\mathsf{k}}^{\star}=k)\perp({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\geqslant k},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\leqslant k},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}})
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\sum_{k\in[k_{r}]}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>k},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}})

(by
Proposition

A.2
, as
{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>k}\perp{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>k}
by the definition of distribution
\mathcal{D}_{r}
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}})

(by chain rule of mutual information,
Fact

A.1
-(
4
))

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}})

(by
Proposition

A.2
, as
{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}}\perp{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
by the definition of distribution
\mathcal{D}_{r}
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}})

(by the chain rule (
Fact

A.1
-(
4
)) and non-negativity of mutual information (
Fact

A.1
-(
2
)))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{(r)}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid{\mathsf{B}}^{(r)}).

(recall that input of
{P}_{X}
(resp.
{P}_{Y}
) simulated by Bob (resp. Alice) in
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
is
A^{(r)}
(resp.
B^{(r)}
))

With both these bounds, we can upper bound the LHS of
Equation

25
and obtain

\displaystyle\textsc{IC}(\pi^{(1)}_{r},\mathcal{D}_{r-1})

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\left(\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{(r)}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid{\mathsf{A}}^{(r)})+\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{(r)}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}\mid{\mathsf{B}}^{(r)})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\textsc{IC}(\pi_{r},\mathcal{D}_{r}),

where the last step follows since the messages communicated in
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
are exactly the messages communicated in the simulation of
\pi_{r}
and since
({\mathsf{A}}^{(r)},{\mathsf{B}}^{(r)})\sim\mathcal{D}_{r}
in
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
by
Observation

5.7
.

We now obtain the following main lemma of this step of the argument:

Lemma 5.9
.

Protocol
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
is an
r
-round protocol for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
where Bob speaks first with communication cost at most
s
, probability of success at least
\delta
, and internal information cost at most
s/k_{r}
when the input is sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
.

Proof.

In protocol
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
, the instance
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
used to simulate
\pi_{r}
is constructed without any communication, so the total number of bits communicated in
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
is at most the total number of bits communicated in
\pi_{r}
, which is
\textsc{CC}(\pi_{r})\leqslant s
as in the statement of
Lemma

5.6
.
Bob takes on the role of
{P}_{X}
in the simulation and thus Bob speaks first.

By construction, protocol
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
outputs the same answer as
\pi_{r}
since the solution to
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})\sim\mathcal{D}_{r}
on search sequence
(k^{\star},k^{\star}_{r-1},k^{\star}_{r-2},\ldots,k^{\star}_{0})
is the same as the solution to the input
(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k^{\star}},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k^{\star}})=(A^{\star},B^{\star})\sim\mathcal{D}_{r-1}
on the search sequence
(k^{\star}_{r-1},k^{\star}_{r-2},\ldots,k^{\star}_{0})
. Therefore,
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
succeeds with probability at least
\delta
.
Finally, the bound on the internal information cost of
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
follows from
Claim

5.8
and the fact that the communication cost of
\pi_{r}
, namely,
s
, is an upper bound of its internal information cost.

Step

(

⁢
i
i

)

: A Low Communication Cost Protocol via Message Compression

In this step, we compress the communication cost of
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
using standard message compression techniques. As a direct corollary of
Proposition

3.13
, we get the following:

Corollary 5.10
.

For any
0<\varepsilon<1
, there exists an
r
-round protocol
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
where Bob speaks first with

\textsc{CC}(\pi^{(2)}_{r})\leqslant c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot(r/\varepsilon)\cdot\left(\frac{s}{k_{r}}+r\right).

and probability of success at least
\delta-\varepsilon
when the input is sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
.

Proof.

We use
Proposition

3.13
on protocol
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
to get protocol
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
such that the simulation fails with probability at most
\varepsilon
. Then, we have that

\displaystyle\textsc{CC}(\pi^{(2)}_{r})

\displaystyle\leqslant c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot\left(r/\varepsilon\cdot\textsc{IC}(\pi^{(1)}_{r},\mathcal{D}_{r-1})+r^{2}/\varepsilon\right)\leqslant c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot(r/\varepsilon)\cdot\left(\frac{s}{k_{r}}+r\right).

(by
Lemma

5.9
)

The simulation of
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
using compressed messages fails with probability at most
\varepsilon
, so
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
succeeds with probability at least
\delta-\varepsilon
, as desired.

Since this step only compresses the messages sent in
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
, protocol
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
is constructed in the same way as
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
with an additional message compression step that uses an independent source of public randomness.
In particular,
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
is a simulation of
\pi_{r}
on an instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r}
, but with communication cost smaller by a
\simeq k_{r}
multiplicative factor and with a slightly smaller probability of success.

Step

(

⁢
i
i
i

)

: An
(r-1)
-Round Protocol via Guessing the First Message

In this final and the most important step, we take the
r
-round protocol
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
where Bob communicates first and we eliminate the first message by making the players
guess
it using public randomness, thus beginning the protocol from the second round.
Therefore, we obtain an
(r-1)
-round protocol
\pi_{r-1}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
where Alice communicates first as required.
This alters the joint distribution of the input and thus affects the guarantees of the protocol (as in
Corollary

5.10
), but we will show that the effect is small.

An
(r-1)
-round protocol
\pi_{r-1}
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
on inputs
(A^{(r-1)},B^{(r-1)})\sim\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
where
(k^{\star}_{r-1},k^{\star}_{r-2},\dots,k^{\star}_{1})
is the given uniform random search sequence
:

(a)

Using
public randomness
, jointly sample the first message
\Pi_{1}
and the search index
k^{\star}
from the protocol
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
. Then, jointly sample the following from
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
conditioned on
\Pi_{1},k^{\star}
:

•

The uniform random permutations
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}
of
[b_{r}]
;

•

Bob’s part of all the
k_{r}^{2}-k_{r}
many off-diagonal sub-instances

B^{\textnormal{{off}}}=(B^{(r-1)}_{i,j}:b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\neq\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r});

•

Alice’s part of the
k^{\star}-1
many special sub-instances
A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k^{\star}}=(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}:1\leqslant k<k^{\star})
;

•

Bob’s part of the
k_{r}-k^{\star}
many special sub-instances
B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>k^{\star}}=(B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}:k^{\star}<k\leqslant k_{r})
.

Let
i^{\star},j^{\star}\in[b_{r}]
be such that
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)=\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)=b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k^{\star}
, which both players can compute since
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}},k^{\star}
are sampled using public randomness.

(b)

Bob takes on the role of player
{P}_{X}
and sets
X[i^{\star},j^{\star}]=B^{\star}
.
Bob
privately samples

X
from
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
conditioned on all the random variables from step

(
a
)

and
X[i^{\star},j^{\star}]
, i.e., the remainder of
{P}_{X}
’s input
A^{(r)}
.

(c)

Alice takes on the role of player
{P}_{Y}
and sets
Y[i^{\star},j^{\star}]=A^{\star}
.
Alice
privately samples

Y
from
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
conditioned on all the variables from step

(
a
)

and
Y[i^{\star},j^{\star}]
, i.e., the remainder of player
{P}_{Y}
’s input
B^{(r)}
.

(d)

The players
{P}_{X}
(
=\text{Bob}
) and
{P}_{Y}
(
=\text{Alice}
) simulate protocol
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
using their respective inputs
A^{(r)}=X,B^{(r)}=(\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}},Y)
and the search sequence
(k^{\star},k^{\star}_{r-1},k^{\star}_{r-2},\dots,k^{\star}_{1})
.
They use
\Pi_{1}
as a guess of the first message sent from
{P}_{X}
to
{P}_{Y}
and thus only simulate
r-1
rounds of
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
starting with the second round, i.e., player
{P}_{Y}
communicates first.

Observe that, similar to the construction of
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
(which is identical to
\pi^{(1)}_{r}
, except for the message compression), protocol
\pi_{r-1}
embeds the input for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}
into a simulated instance
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
.
Then, the players simulate a run of
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
using
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
where, to remove the first round of communication, the first message is guessed using public randomness.
However, due to the random guessing of the first message, the simulated instance is no longer distributed according to
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
(recall
Observation

5.7
), i.e., the guarantees of
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
do not hold for
\pi_{r-1}
.
Despite this, we show that the simulated instance and first message are statistically close to being distributed as they are in
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
and thus similar guarantees hold.

Let
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{fake}}}
denote the joint distribution of
({\mathsf{A}}^{(r)},{\mathsf{B}}^{(r)},{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star})
as it is in
\pi_{r-1}
and let
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}
denote the joint distribution of
({\mathsf{A}}^{(r)},{\mathsf{B}}^{(r)},{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star})
as it is in
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
.
Let

{\mathsf{R}}_{a}:=({\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

denote the public randomness used in step

(
a
)

of
\pi_{r-1}
.
We show in
Claim

5.11
that the distributions
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{fake}}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}
only differ in the way that the input
({\mathsf{A}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\star})=({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})
is sampled.

Claim 5.11
.

We have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{fake}}}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{R}}_{a})\times~~~\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})~~~\!\times\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})\times\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{R}}_{a})\times\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a})\times\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})\times\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}).

Proof.

We first consider
({\mathsf{A}}^{(r)},{\mathsf{B}}^{(r)},{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star})
directly as it is sampled in
\pi_{r-1}
.
The random variable
{\mathsf{R}}_{a}
in
\pi_{r-1}
is jointly sampled in step

(
a
)

.
Then, Alice and Bob embed
({\mathsf{A}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\star})\sim\mathcal{D}_{r-1}
as
{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}={\mathsf{A}}^{\star}
and
{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}={\mathsf{B}}^{\star}
, i.e.,
({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})\sim\mathcal{D}_{r-1}
, which is
not
conditioned on
{\mathsf{R}}_{a}
.
Finally, Bob and Alice privately sample the remainder of
X
and
Y
(and thus the remainder of
A^{(r)}
and
B^{(r)}
), respectively, which is only conditioned on
{\mathsf{R}}_{a}
and their respective embedded inputs. This defines
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{fake}}}
.

We then obtain
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}
from
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{fake}}}
by including all previously sampled random variables in the conditioning of the subsequently sampled random variables. That is, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{R}}_{a})

\displaystyle\times\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a})

\displaystyle\times\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

\displaystyle\times\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\geqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}).

It is easy to verify that this is exactly the distribution of
({\mathsf{A}}^{(r)},{\mathsf{B}}^{(r)},{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star})
as it is in
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
.

Finally, we simplify the third and fourth terms in
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}
to show that they are identical to the corresponding terms in
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{fake}}}
, which follows directly from the claims that

({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})\perp{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}}\perp{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\geqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},

respectively.
To prove these claims, it is sufficient to bound the corresponding mutual information terms as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\leqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(by the chain rule (
Fact

A.1
-(
4
)) and non-negativity of mutual information (
Fact

A.1
-(
2
)))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\leqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}}\mid{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\leqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(by the definition of
{\mathsf{R}}_{a}
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\leqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}},{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\leqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(by the chain rule (
Fact

A.1
-(
4
)) and non-negativity of mutual information (
Fact

A.1
-(
2
)))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\leqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\leqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(as
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}
is fixed by
{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
)

\displaystyle=0,

(by
Fact

A.1
-(
2
) and construction of distribution
\mathcal{D}_{r}
)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}}\,;{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\geqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}},{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\,;{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\geqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\geqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\leqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(by the chain rule (
Fact

A.1
-(
4
)) and non-negativity of mutual information (
Fact

A.1
-(
2
)))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}}\,;{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\geqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\geqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\leqslant{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(as
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}
is fixed by
{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
)

\displaystyle=0.

(by
Fact

A.1
-(
2
) and construction of distribution
\mathcal{D}_{r}
)

This completes the proof.

We note here that the simulated instance
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
in
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}
is sampled according to
\mathcal{D}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
(recall
Observation

5.7
).
However, this is not true in
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{fake}}}
since
({\mathsf{A}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\star})=({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})
is sampled according to
=\mathcal{D}_{r-1}
instead of
\mathcal{D}_{r}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a}
.
Thus, to bound the total variation distance between
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{fake}}}
, we show that the information revealed by the random variable

{\mathsf{R}}_{a}

about the input
({\mathsf{A}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\star})
in
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
, i.e., under distribution
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}
, is not too large.
We need an independence claim (
Claim

5.12
) and then a crucial bound on the information carried by the first message (
Claim

5.13
).

Claim 5.12
.

{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\perp{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}.

Proof.

Again, we bound the mutual information between the terms. Let
{\mathsf{R}}_{\text{comp}}
represent the independent source of public randomness used in the message compression in step

(

⁢
i
i

)

. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(moving
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}
by chain rule in
Fact

A.1
-(
4
), and non-negativity of mutual information
Fact

A.1
-(
2
))

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}},{\mathsf{R}}_{\text{comp}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(by data processing inequality in
Fact

A.1
-(
5
),
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}
is fixed by
{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{R}}_{\text{comp}}
)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(since
{\mathsf{R}}_{\text{comp}}
is an entirely independent source of randomness)

\displaystyle\leqslant 0.\penalty\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 15.00002pt\penalty\vrule height=7.5pt,width=5.0pt,depth=2.5pt

(by
Fact

A.1
-(
2
) and construction of
\mathcal{D}_{r}
)

The following claim is the heart of the entire proof.

Claim 5.13
.

For any
k\in[k_{r}]
,

\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>k})\leqslant\frac{s}{b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}.

Proof.

Let
\rho_{\textnormal{{r}}},\rho_{\textnormal{{c}}}
be the inverse of
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}
, and let
\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{c}}}
denote the random variables corresponding to
\rho_{\textnormal{{r}}},\rho_{\textnormal{{c}}}
respectively.
For ease of exposition, we represent any permutation
\rho
on
[b_{r}]
as an ordered set
S:=\{\rho(\ell):\ell\in[b_{r}]\}
with the ordering that
\rho(\ell)<\rho(\ell^{\prime})
for
\ell<\ell^{\prime}
.
Using this representation, we allow for partial definitions of permutations, i.e., ordered subsets
T\subset S
.
In particular, we require the following partial definitions of
\rho_{\textnormal{{r}}}
and
\rho_{\textnormal{{c}}}
:

•

Let
T_{\textnormal{{row}}}
be an ordered set

T_{\textnormal{{row}}}=\left\{\rho_{\textnormal{{r}}}(\ell)\mid b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\ell\leqslant b_{r},\ell\neq b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k\right\}

of size
b_{r}\cdot(1-\alpha)-1
, which is the set of rows that corresponds to
B^{\textnormal{{off}}}
except for the row corresponding to
{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}
. Let
{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}}
denote its random variable.

•

Let
T_{\textnormal{{col}}}
be an ordered set

T_{\textnormal{{col}}}=\left\{\rho_{\textnormal{{c}}}(\ell)\mid b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\ell\leqslant b_{r},\ell\neq b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k\right\}

of size
b_{r}\cdot(1-\alpha)-1
, which is the set of columns that corresponds to
B^{\textnormal{{off}}}
except for the column corresponding to
{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}
. Let
{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}}
denote its random variable.

Figure

10
.

Figure 10
:
An illustration of the partial definitions
T_{\textnormal{{row}}}
and
T_{\textnormal{{col}}}
for an instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
where
b_{r}=7
,
\alpha=2/7
, and
k=2
. In this figure, the
b_{r}\times b_{r}
matrix is after applying the respective permutations of rows and columns using
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}
and
\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}
held by Bob.
In each position of the matrix, the corresponding input is the
B^{\prime}s
from each instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
that is held by Alice.

T_{\textnormal{{row}}}
and
T_{\textnormal{{col}}}
are partial definitions of the permutations
\rho_{\textnormal{{r}}}
and
\rho_{\textnormal{{c}}}
, i.e., the inverses of
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}
and
\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}
, respectively.

T_{\textnormal{{row}}}
corresponds to fixing the rows filled red and
T_{\textnormal{{col}}}
corresponds to fixing the columns filled blue, which implies fixing positions filled purple. The unfilled (white) positions remain unrestricted by
T_{\textnormal{{row}}},T_{\textnormal{{col}}}
, i.e., the green outlined special position
B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}
could still (uniformly) be in any of the unfilled positions when conditioned on
T_{\textnormal{{row}}},T_{\textnormal{{col}}}
(instead of
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}
).

This now allows us to write the indices
i,j\in[b_{r}]
of the instances corresponding to
{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
and
{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}}
using
\rho_{\textnormal{{r}}},\rho_{\textnormal{{c}}},T_{\textnormal{{row}}},T_{\textnormal{{col}}}
:

(i)

For any
\ell\in[k_{r}]
,
{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{\ell}
is the same as the random variable
{\mathsf{B}}_{\rho_{\textnormal{{r}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+\ell),\rho_{\textnormal{{c}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+\ell)}

where we omit the superscript
r-1
as it is clear from context;

(ii)

{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>k}
is the union of combinatorial rectangles formed by

({\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}}\times{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}}),\quad({\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}}\times\left\{\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{c}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k)\right\}),\quad(\left\{\rho_{\textnormal{{r}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k)\right\}\times{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}}).

Finally, let
{\mathsf{Z}}
be defined as,

{\mathsf{Z}}:=({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>k}).

With these at hand, we can start bounding the mutual information term in the claim. Firstly, it is easy to see that,

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>k})=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{Z}}),

by definition of
{\mathsf{Z}}
.

We need the following intermediate claim for our proof.

Claim 5.14
.

\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k},{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},{\mathsf{Z}}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}})=\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k},{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},{\mathsf{Z}}\mid{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}},\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k),\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{c}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k)).

Proof.

Let
\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}([b_{r}\cdot\alpha])
and
\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{c}}}([b_{r}\cdot\alpha])
denote the random variables
\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)
and
\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{c}}}(i)
for all
i\in[b_{r}\cdot\alpha]
respectively.
We know that random variable
\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
is fixed by
\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
, as they are inverses of each other. Random variable
\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
is in turn fixed by all the values each element of
[b_{r}]
is mapped to, i.e.,
\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)
for
i\in[b_{r}]
.

Hence, it is sufficient to argue that,

({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k},{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},{\mathsf{Z}})\perp\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}([b_{r}\cdot\alpha]),\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{c}}}([b_{r}\cdot\alpha])\mid{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}},\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k),\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{c}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k),

to prove the claim.
We know that
{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k},{\mathsf{Z}}
is the random variable corresponding to the instances in
A^{(r)}
inside the combinatorial rectangle

({\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}}\cup\{\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k)\})\times({\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}}\cup\{\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{c}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k)\}).

The entirety of this rectangle is fixed by the conditioning.

Proving the claim amounts to proving that the joint distribution of
B^{(r-1)}_{i,j}
for a fixed set of positions of
A^{(r)}
(the ones inside the rectangle fixed by conditioning), and the transcript
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}
is independent of the
permutation
of the rest of the positions of
A^{(r)}
. This is true because
{P}_{X}
, who sends
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}
, does not have access to either of the permutations
\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}
or
\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{c}}}
(held by
{P}_{Y}
).

Claim

5.14

We can continue to bound the mutual information now.

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{Z}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}},\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k),\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{c}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k))

(by
Claim

5.14
)

\displaystyle=\Exp_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T_{\textnormal{{row}}},T_{\textnormal{{col}}})\\
\sim({\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}})\end{subarray}}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}}=T_{\textnormal{{row}}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}}=T_{\textnormal{{col}}},\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k),\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{c}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k))

(by definition of conditional mutual information)

\displaystyle\underset{\textnormal{(1)}}{\leqslant}\frac{1}{b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}\Exp_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T_{\textnormal{{row}}},T_{\textnormal{{col}}})\\
\sim({\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[b_{r}]\setminus{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},\\
j\in[b_{r}]\setminus{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}_{i,j}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid{\mathsf{Z}},T_{\textnormal{{row}}},T_{\textnormal{{col}}},\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k)=i,\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{c}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k)=j)

\displaystyle\underset{\textnormal{(2)}}{=}\frac{1}{b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}\Exp_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T_{\textnormal{{row}}},T_{\textnormal{{col}}})\\
\sim({\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}})\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in[b_{r}]\setminus{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},j\in[b_{r}]\setminus{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}}}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}_{i,j}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}}=T_{\textnormal{{row}}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}}=T_{\textnormal{{col}}})

\displaystyle\underset{\textnormal{(3)}}{=}\frac{1}{b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}\sum_{i\in[b_{r}]\setminus{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},j\in[b_{r}]\setminus{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}}}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}_{i,j}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}})

where

(1)
holds by the definition of conditional mutual information since, conditioned on any
{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}}=T_{\textnormal{{row}}}
and
{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}}=T_{\textnormal{{col}}}
, the values of
\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k)
and
\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{c}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k)
are uniformly chosen from
[b_{r}]\backslash T_{\textnormal{{row}}}
and
[b_{r}]\backslash T_{\textnormal{{col}}}
(each of size
b_{r}\cdot\alpha+1
), respectively;

(2)
holds because, conditioned on any choice of
{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}}=T_{\textnormal{{row}}}
and
{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}}=T_{\textnormal{{col}}}
, the event
\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{c}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k)=j,\bm{\rho}_{\textnormal{{r}}}(b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k)=i
is independent of
({\mathsf{B}}_{i,j},{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},{\mathsf{Z}})
; and

(3)
holds by the linearity of expectation and the definition of conditional mutual information.

We re-index both the sets
[b_{r}]\setminus{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}}
and
[b_{r}]\setminus{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}}
as
[b_{r}\cdot\alpha+1]
for ease of exposition just for this final part of the proof.
We know that
{\mathsf{B}}_{i,j}
for any
i,j\in[b_{r}]
are sampled independently of each other in distribution
\mathcal{D}_{r}
.
Hence, using
Proposition

A.2
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}\sum_{i\in[b_{r}]\setminus{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},j\in[b_{r}]\setminus{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}}}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}_{i,j}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}\sum_{i\in[b_{r}\cdot\alpha+1],j\in[b_{r}\cdot\alpha+1]}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}_{i,j}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid{\mathsf{B}}_{i,>j},{\mathsf{B}}_{>i},{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}_{1,1},\ldots,{\mathsf{B}}_{1,b_{r}\cdot\alpha+1},{\mathsf{B}}_{2,1},\ldots,{\mathsf{B}}_{b_{r}\cdot\alpha+1,b_{r}\cdot\alpha+1}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}})

(by chain rule of mutual information,
Fact

A.1
-(
4
))

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{(r)}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}})

(by the chain rule (
Fact

A.1
-(
4
)) and non-negativity of mutual information (
Fact

A.1
-(
2
)))

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{s}{b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}.

(as
\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{(r)}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{row}}},{\mathsf{T}}_{\textnormal{{col}}})\leqslant\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1})\leqslant s
by
Fact

A.1
-(
1
))

This proves the claim.

We now use these claims to show that the information revealed by random variable
{\mathsf{R}}_{a}
about the input
({\mathsf{A}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\star})
is not too large in
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}
.

Claim 5.15
.

\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{R}}_{a})\leqslant\frac{s}{b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}.

Proof.

First, by chain rule of mutual information (
Fact

A.1
-(
4
)), we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{R}}_{a})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{R}}_{a})+\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{R}}_{a}\mid{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}).

We can prove that the second term is zero as follows: Let
{\mathsf{R}}_{\text{comp}}
represent the independent source of public randomness used in the message compression in step

(

⁢
i
i

)

. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{R}}_{a}\mid{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(by expanding
{\mathsf{R}}_{a}
)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})+\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(by chain rule in
Fact

A.1
-(
