))

\displaystyle=0+\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}).

(by
Fact

A.1
-(
2
) and construction of
\mathcal{D}_{r}
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{R}}_{\text{comp}}\mid{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}).

(by data processing inequality
Fact

A.1
-(
5
) as
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}
is fixed by
{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}},{\mathsf{R}}_{\text{comp}}
)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{rest}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}).

(since
{\mathsf{R}}_{\text{comp}}
is an entirely independent source of randomness)

\displaystyle=0.

(by
Fact

A.1
-(
2
) and construction of
\mathcal{D}_{r}
)

Therefore, an upper bound on
\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{R}}_{a})
is an upper bound on
\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{R}}_{a})
. We have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{R}}_{a})

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt\leqslant\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{R}}_{a},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(by the chain rule (
Fact

A.1
-(
4
)) and non-negativity of mutual information (
Fact

A.1
-(
2
)))

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(by expanding
{\mathsf{R}}_{a}
)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(by chain rule in
Fact

A.1
-(
4
))

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt=0+\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(by
Fact

A.1
-(
2
) and construction of
\mathcal{D}_{r}
)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt\leqslant\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})

(removing
{\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}
in the conditioning by
Proposition

A.3
and
Claim

5.12
)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt=\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\sum_{k\in[k_{r}]}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}=k,{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>k},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k})

(by the definition of conditional mutual information)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt=\frac{1}{k_{r}}\cdot\sum_{k\in[k_{r}]}\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\,;{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1}\mid\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{>k},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{<k})

(as event
{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}=k
is independent of
({\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{off}}},{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{r}}},\bm{\sigma}_{\textnormal{{c}}})
(see proof of
Claim

5.8
))

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt\leqslant\frac{s}{b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}},

by applying
Claim

5.13
to bound each term, completing the proof.

We are now ready to show that
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{fake}}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}
are statistically close.

Lemma 5.16
.

\|\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}-\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{fake}}}\|_{\mathrm{tvd}}\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{s}{2\cdot b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}}.

Proof.

Firstly, we use weak chain rule over total variation distance (
Fact

A.6
) using the definitions of distributions
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{fake}}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}
established in
Claim

5.11
to obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}-\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{fake}}}\|_{\mathrm{tvd}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Exp_{R\sim{\mathsf{R}}_{a}}\|\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a}=R)-\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}})\|_{\mathrm{tvd}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Exp_{R\sim{\mathsf{R}}_{a}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbb{D}(\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a}=R)~||~\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}))}

(by Pinsker’s inequality in
Fact

A.7
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\Exp_{R\sim{\mathsf{R}}_{a}}\mathbb{D}(\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\mid{\mathsf{R}}_{a}=R)~||~\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}))}

(by Jensen’s inequality as the function
\sqrt{\cdot}
is concave)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}},{\mathsf{B}}^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{{\mathsf{k}}^{\star}}\,;{\mathsf{R}}_{a})}

(by the relation between mutual information and KL-divergence in
Fact

A.4
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{s}{2\cdot b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}},

(by
Claim

5.15
)

proving the lemma.

Finally, we complete the proof of the main round-elimination argument.

Proof of
Lemma

5.6
.

By construction, we know that the total communication of protocol
\pi_{r-1}
is upper bounded by the communication of protocol
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
(since the messages of the former are a subset of the ones for the latter).
Using
Corollary

5.10
, we get that the communication of
\pi_{r-1}
is at most

\textsc{CC}(\pi_{r-1})\leqslant c_{\textsc{jpy}}\cdot(r/\varepsilon)\cdot\left(\frac{s}{k_{r}}+r\right).

Furthermore, by construction, protocol
\pi_{r-1}
is an
(r-1)
-round protocol where Alice communicates first.

We know, again from
Corollary

5.10
, that the probability of success of
\pi^{(2)}_{r}
is at least
\delta-\varepsilon
when the simulated input
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
is sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r}
, i.e., the random variables follow distribution
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}
.
However, in
\pi_{r-1}
, due to the fact that we sample the first round message at random, the simulated input is only statistically close to being sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r}
, i.e., the random variables follow distribution
\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{fake}}}
.
Therefore, the probability of success of
\pi_{r-1}
, using
Fact

A.5
, is bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\textnormal{suc}(\pi_{r-1})

\displaystyle\geqslant\Prob\left(\pi^{(2)}_{r}\textnormal{~succeeds on input $\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}$}\right)-\|\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{real}}}-\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{{fake}}}\|_{\mathrm{tvd}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\delta-\varepsilon-\sqrt{\frac{s}{2\cdot b_{r}^{2}\cdot\alpha^{2}}}.

Lastly, to get a deterministic protocol out of
\pi_{r-1}
, we use an averaging argument and fix the random bits of
\pi_{r-1}
so that we have the same performance guarantee over the input distribution
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
. This
gives us the desired
(r-1)
-round deterministic protocol for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}(n_{r},\alpha)
when the players’ inputs are sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r-1}(n_{r-1},\alpha)
, and the search sequence is uniformly random.

5.2
Reduction to Bipartite Matching in Dynamic Streams

In this section, we prove the connection between maximum bipartite matching in the dynamic streaming model and the Augmented Hidden Matrices (
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
) problem, which is presented as
Lemma

5.2
.
We repeat this lemma for the convenience of the reader here.

Lemma

5.2
(restated).

For any sufficiently large
n_{r}\in\mathbb{N}
, integers
p,s\geqslant 1
, and number
\beta\geqslant 1
, let
\mathcal{A}
by any
p
-pass
s
-space
dynamic streaming algorithm that computes a
\beta
-approximate maximum matching on a
(4n_{r})
-vertex bipartite graphs with probability of success at least
1-1/\mbox{\rm poly}{(n)}
.
Then, for
r=2p-1
and
\alpha=1/(4\cdot\beta\cdot r)
, there exists an
r
-round protocol
\pi
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
with
probability of success

\textnormal{suc}(\pi)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{3\beta}\right)

and
communication cost

\textsc{CC}(\pi)\leqslant r\cdot s+O(r\cdot n_{r}\log{(n_{r})}).

The key to proving
Lemma

5.2
is to construct a bipartite graph
G=(L\cup R,E_{\textnormal{ins}}\backslash E_{\textnormal{del}})
, which is defined by edge insertions
E_{\textnormal{ins}}
and deletions
E_{\textnormal{del}}
,
from any input instance for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
with the following property. An
O(1)
-approximate maximum bipartite matching in
G
can be used to solve
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
on the
search sequence with a non-trivial advantage over randomly guessing the answer.

5.2.1
The Bipartite Graph Construction using
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}

Let us first recall the important aspects of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
.
It is defined recursively using
b_{r}^{2}
many
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-1}
sub-instances, which are denoted
(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
for
i,j\in[b_{r}]
(see
Figure

9
in
Section

5.1
).
An instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
identifies
k_{r}
many special sub-instances
(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}})
.
Each of these has its own
k_{r-1}
many special (sub-)sub-instances and, continuing this, ultimately corresponds to the
k_{r}\cdot k_{r-1}\cdot\ldots\cdot k_{1}
many special base instances, where each one corresponds to a single bit as they are instances of
\mathsf{AHM}_{0}
.
Then, given the uniform random search sequence, which is provided at the end of a protocol, solving
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
requires solving a uniform random special base instance.

We begin by showing how the base instances (or bits) are represented in our graph.
We will use the following basic bipartite graph construction on four vertices, called a
bit graph
, to encode bits in our construction of
G
.

Definition 5.17

(Bit Graph)
.

Given a bit
x\in\{0,1\}
and a graph
G=(L\cup R,E)
with
L=\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}
and
R=\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
, graph
G
is said to be the bit graph of
x
if

•

E=\{(\ell^{1},r^{1}),(\ell^{2},r^{2})\}
when
x=0
, and
E=\{(\ell^{1},r^{2}),(\ell^{2},r^{1})\}
when
x=1
.

The bit
x
can be identified by either of the edges in
E
. See
Figure

11
for an illustration.

\ell^{2}
\ell^{1}
r^{2}
r^{1}

(a)

When the bit
x=0
.

\ell^{2}
\ell^{1}
r^{2}
r^{1}

(b)

When the bit
x=1
.

Figure 11
:
An illustration of the bit graph on vertices corresponding to a bit
x\in\{0,1\}
.

With that, given an instance
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
, we give the following recursive procedure that separately encodes
A^{(r)}
and
B^{(r)}
as the edges of a bipartite graph on the vertex sets
L
and
R
, where,

\displaystyle L=\{\ell^{1}_{t},\ell^{2}_{t}\mid t\in[n_{r}]\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad R=\{r^{1}_{t},r^{2}_{t}\mid t\in[n_{r}]\}.

For ease of notation, we let

L[i]=\left\{\ell^{1}_{t},\ell^{2}_{t}\mid(i-1)\cdot n_{r-1}<t\leqslant i\cdot n_{r-1}\right\}\subseteq L

for
i\in[b_{r}]
, and we let
R[j]\subseteq R
for
j\in[b_{r}]
be defined similarly.
The edges we add corresponding to the sub-instance
(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
will be only across vertices in
L[i]
and
R[j]
.

We also use
\bm{1}(t)
and
\bm{0}(t)
to denote the
t\times t
matrix of all ones and all zeroes, respectively.
The construction of the graph is as follows.

\textnormal{{edges}}(r,Z,L,R)
where
Z\in\{A^{(r)},B^{(r)},\bm{1}(n_{r}),\bm{0}(n_{r})\}
with
\left|{L}\right|=\left|{R}\right|=2n_{r}
:

•

For
\mathbf{r=0}
.

–

When
Z\in\{0,1\}
, return the edges of the bit graph corresponding to
Z
.

–

When
Z=\emptyset
, return the empty set of edges.

•

For
\mathbf{r\geqslant 1}
.

–

When
Z\in\{\bm{1}(n_{r}),\bm{0}(n_{r})\}
, return the edges

\bigcup_{i,j\in[b_{r}]}\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,Z^{\prime},L[i],R[j])

where
Z^{\prime}
is the
n_{r-1}\times n_{r-1}
matrix with all its entries the same as the entries in
Z
.

–

When
{Z=A^{(r)}}
, return the edges

\bigcup_{i,j\in[b_{r}]}\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,B^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j]).

–

When
{Z=B^{(r)}}
, return the edges
19
19

19

This procedure ignores
A^{\textnormal{{off}}}=(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j}:b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\neq\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r})
.

\displaystyle\Big(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[b_{r}]\\
b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\neq\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}\end{subarray}}\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,B^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j])\Big)

\displaystyle\cup\Big(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[b_{r}]\\
b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)=\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}\end{subarray}}\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j])\Big).

If
r
is even, in addition to the above, return the edges

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[b_{r}]\\
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\leqslant b_{r}\cdot\alpha\text{~or~}\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}\cdot\alpha\end{subarray}}\big(\textnormal{{edges}}(0,\bm{1}(n_{r-1}),L[i],R[j])\cup\textnormal{{edges}}(0,\bm{0}(n_{r-1}),L[i],R[j])\big).

See
Figure

12
for an illustration.

Note that this procedure does not yet define the graph
G
. However, it allows Alice and Bob to construct edges (insertions or deletions) from their respective inputs, which we now show can be done without any communication.

Claim 5.18
.

For any
r\geqslant 0
, given an instance
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
, Alice and Bob can compute
\textnormal{{edges}}(r,A^{(r)},L,R)
and
\textnormal{{edges}}(r,B^{(r)},L,R)
, respectively, without any communication.

Proof.

The proof of the claim is by induction on
r
where the base case when
r=0
is trivial, i.e., both
\textnormal{{edges}}(0,A^{(0)},L,R)
and
\textnormal{{edges}}(0,B^{(0)},L,R)
can be constructed without communication.

For any
r\geqslant 1
, the player holding
A^{(r)}
has access to all
B^{(r-1)}_{i,j}
for
i,j\in[b_{r}]
. Thus, the player can construct the required set of edges
\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,B^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j])
for all
i,j\in[b_{r}]
without any communication by the induction hypothesis.
The player holding
B^{(r)}
has access to permutations
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}
and the following:

•

B^{(r-1)}_{i,j}
for
b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\neq\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}
and thus the player can construct construct the required

\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,B^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j])

without any communication by the induction hypothesis; and

•

A^{(r-1)}_{i,j}
when
b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)=\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}
and thus the player can construct

\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j])

without any communication, again, by the induction hypothesis.

Additionally, when
r
is even, this player constructs

\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,\bm{1}(n_{r-1}),L[i],R[j])\quad\text{and}\quad\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,\bm{0}(n_{r-1}),L[i],R[j])

when
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\leqslant b_{r}\cdot\alpha
or
\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}\cdot\alpha
, which is trivial and does not need any communication since it only requires the knowledge of
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}
.

L[1]
\vdots
L[b_{r}]
R[1]
\vdots
R[b_{r}]

An illustration of Alice’s edges corresponding to
A^{(r)}
. An orange edge between
L[i]
and
R[j]
for each
i,j\in[b_{r}]
represents the set of edges corresponding to the input
B^{(r-1)}_{i,j}
held by Alice.

L[\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(1)]
\vdots
L[\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(b_{r})]

\vdots

(b)

An illustration of Bob’s edges corresponding to
B^{(r)}
. The vertex groups are represented after the permutations
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}
held by Bob. Each orange edge represents the edges corresponding to each off-diagonal sub-instances
B^{\textnormal{{off}}}
held by Bob. Each blue edge represents the edges corresponding to the special sub-instances
A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
held by Bob. Each dashed gray edge represents the set of no edges when
r
is odd and the set of all possible edges when
r
is even.

Figure 12
:
Illustrations of Alice’s edges
\textnormal{{edges}}(r,A^{(r)},L,R)
(
Figure

12(a)
) and Bob’s edges
\textnormal{{edges}}(r,B^{(r)},L,R)
(
Figure

12(b)
). In both illustrations, the nodes on the left and right corresponds to vertex groups
L[i]
and
R[j]
for each
i,j\in[b_{r}]
, respectively.
An edge between vertex groups
L[i]
and
R[j]
represents the set of edges corresponding to the instance
(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
.

Using the above recursive definition, we obtain the main procedure for constructing a bipartite graph
G
from any instance
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
.

One player will be solely responsible for adding edges to
G
, and the other player only deletes edges from
G
. The particular roles of Alice and Bob depends on the parity of
r
.

\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
for instance
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
:

•

When
r
is
odd
, Alice inserts the edges
E_{\textnormal{ins}}=\textnormal{{edges}}(r,A^{(r)},L,R)
and Bob deletes the edges
E_{\textnormal{del}}=\textnormal{{edges}}(r,B^{(r)},L,R)
;

•

When
r
is
even
, Bob inserts the edges
E_{\textnormal{ins}}=\textnormal{{edges}}(r,B^{(r)},L,R)
and Alice deletes the edges
E_{\textnormal{del}}=\textnormal{{edges}}(r,A^{(r)},L,R)
.

Return graph
G=(L\cup R,E_{\textnormal{ins}}\backslash E_{\textnormal{del}})
. See
Figure

13
for an illustration of
G
.

L[\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(1)]
\vdots
L[\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(b_{r})]
R[\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(1)]
\vdots
R[\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(b_{r})]
b_{r}\cdot(1-\alpha)
b_{r}\cdot(1-\alpha)
b_{r}\cdot\alpha

Figure 13
:
An illustration of a graph
G=\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
constructed from an instance
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
.
The nodes on the left and right corresponds to vertex groups
L[i]
and
R[j]
for each
i,j\in[b_{r}]
and are ordered after applying the permutations
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}
and
\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}
, respectively.
An edge between vertex groups
L[i]
and
R[j]
represents the set of edges corresponding to the instance
(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
, where a lack of edge implies that all edges inserted have also been deleted, i.e., the off-diagonal sub-instances
(A^{\textnormal{{off}}},B^{\textnormal{{off}}})
.
Each purple edge represents the edges that correspond to each special sub-instance in
(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}})
. The remaining thick gray edges represent the edges corresponding to sub-instances in
B^{\textnormal{{rest}}}
.
The box represents the subgraph of
G
induced by the vertices
L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}\cup R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
. See
Figure

9
for a comparison to
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
.

Now, let us show that the instances we create are indeed valid graphs.

Claim 5.19
.

For any
r\geqslant 0
, the graph
G=(L\cup R,E_{\textnormal{ins}}\backslash E_{\textnormal{del}})=\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
is a valid graph, namely,
E_{\textnormal{del}}\subseteq E_{\textnormal{ins}}
.

Proof.

By construction, the player that inserts the edges and the player that deletes the edges depends on the parity of
r
.
When
r
is odd,
E_{\textnormal{ins}}=\textnormal{{edges}}(r,A^{(r)},L,R)
and
E_{\textnormal{del}}=\textnormal{{edges}}(r,B^{(r)},L,R)
, and when
r
is even, the roles are reversed.
Hence, we prove the claim by induction on
r
where, when
r
is odd,

\textnormal{{edges}}(r,B^{(r)},L,R)\subset\textnormal{{edges}}(r,A^{(r)},L,R),

, and when
r
is even, the vice-versa holds.

When
r=0
(even case), there are no edge deletions since
\textnormal{{edges}}(0,A^{(0)},L,R)=\emptyset
, so the claim vacuously holds.
When
r\geqslant 1
, we consider the cases where
r
is odd and even separately.

Odd
r
.

We consider the following groupings of the edges separately:

•

When
b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\neq\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}
, the edge set
\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,B^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j])
held by Bob is directly contained in the edges
\textnormal{{edges}}(r,A^{(r)},L,R)
held by Alice.

•

When
b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)=\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}
, Bob holds the edge set
\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j])
and Alice holds the edge set
\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,B^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j])
.
Immediately by the induction hypothesis, since
r-1
is even, we have that
\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j])\subseteq\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,B^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j])
.

Even
r
.

Here, we have three different cases:

•

When
b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\neq\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}
, the edges
\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,B^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j])
held by Alice are directly contained in the edges
\textnormal{{edges}}(r,B^{(r)},L,R)
by Bob.

•

When
b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)=\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}
, by the induction hypothesis as
r-1
is odd, we have that
\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,B^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j])\subseteq\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j])
and are thus contained in
\textnormal{{edges}}(r,B^{(r)},L,R)
.

•

When
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\leqslant b_{r}\cdot\alpha
or
\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}\cdot\alpha
, the edges
\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,B^{(r-1)}_{i,j},L[i],R[j])
are trivially a subset of
\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,\bm{1}(n_{r-1}),L[i],R[j])\cup\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,\bm{0}(n_{r-1}),L[i],R[j])
, which are all included in
\textnormal{{edges}}(r,B^{(r)},L,R)
since
r
is even.

Overall, we have that
E_{\textnormal{del}}\subseteq E_{\textnormal{ins}}
for any choice of
r\geqslant 0
and thus
G=(L\cup R,E_{\textnormal{ins}}\backslash E_{\textnormal{del}})=\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
is a valid graph.

With that, we have that the players can take an instance
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
and construct a valid graph
G=(L\cup R,E_{\textnormal{ins}}\backslash E_{\textnormal{del}})
without any communication. In the next section, we will show some structural properties of
G
that relate it to
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
.

5.2.2
Properties of the Constructed Graph

We need some notation before we begin proving the desirable properties of our construction.
We define the following sets of special vertices: For
i,j\in[b_{r}]
,

\displaystyle L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}:=L[i]\text{~when $\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)=b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k$}\qquad\text{and}\qquad R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}:=R[j]\text{~when $\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)=b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k$}

for all
k\in[k_{r}]
, and

\displaystyle L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}:=\bigcup_{k\in[k_{r}]}L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\qquad\text{and}\qquad R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}:=\bigcup_{k\in[k_{r}]}R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}.

See
Figure

13
for an illustration of the special vertices.
In the following claim, we show that the special vertices do, in fact, correspond to the special sub-instances
(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}})
of
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
.

Claim 5.20
.

For
r\geqslant 1
, the subgraph of
G=\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
induced by the vertices
L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}\cup R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
is exactly the vertex-disjoint union of graphs
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k})
for all
k\in[k_{r}]
.

Proof.

We only prove the claim when
r
is odd, as the case when
r
is even follows similarly.

Observe first that the only possible edges between
L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
and
R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
are the edges between
L[i]
and
R[j]
corresponding to
(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
for any
i,j\in[b_{r}]
with
b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i),\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}
.
Considering only these edges, we split our analysis into two cases:

•

When
b_{r}\cdot\alpha<\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\neq\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}
, Alice adds the edges corresponding to
B^{(r-1)}_{i,j}
,
which is exactly the set of edges deleted by Bob.
Hence, none of these edges are present in
G
.

•

When
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)=\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)=b_{r}\cdot\alpha+k
for
k\in[k_{r}]
, Alice adds the edges corresponding to
B^{(r-1)}_{i,j}
,
whereas Bob deletes the edges corresponding to
A^{(r-1)}_{i,j}
.
As
r-1
is even, these edge insertions and deletions exactly correspond to
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
.
It then follows that the subgraph of
G
induced by
L[i]\cup R[j]=L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}\cup R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k}
is an exact copy of
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k})
.

Finally, for any
k_{1},k_{2}\in[k_{r}]
,
L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k_{1}}\cup R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k_{1}}
is disjoint from
L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k_{2}}\cup R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k_{2}}
. Hence, the subgraph induced by
L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}\cup R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
is a vertex-disjoint union of the graphs
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{k})
for
k\in[k_{r}]
, proving the claim.

To highlight another key structural property in our construction of
G
, recall from
Observation

5.4
that any instance
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
defines
k_{r}\cdot k_{r-1}\cdot\ldots\cdot k_{1}
many special base instances.
We identify these special base instances and their corresponding sets of special base vertices in
L
and
R
using the tuples in
[k_{r}]\times[k_{r-1}]\times\ldots\times[k_{1}]
. Formally, we have the following notation:

Notation.

Any tuple
s=(s_{r},s_{r-1},\ldots,s_{1})
identifies the special sub-instance
(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r}},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r}})
of
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
, then the special sub-instance
(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r-1}},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r-1}})
inside
(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r}},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r}})
, and so on until finally pointing to a single special base instance
(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{1}},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{1}})
.
We denote this special base instance as
(A^{*}_{s},B^{*}_{s})
.
Furthermore, we use
(A^{*},B^{*})
to denote the collection of special base instances
(A^{*}_{s},B^{*}_{s})
for all
s=(s_{r},s_{r-1},\ldots,s_{1})\in[k_{r}]\times[k_{r-1}]\times\ldots\times[k_{1}]
.

Any tuple
s=(s_{r},s_{r-1},\ldots,s_{1})
also identifies the sets of vertices
L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r}}\subseteq L
and
R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r}}\subseteq R
, then the sets
L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r-1}}\subseteq L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r}}
and
R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r-1}}\subseteq R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r}}
, and so on until identifying the sets of special base vertices
L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{1}}\subseteq L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{2}}
in
L
and
R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{1}}\subseteq R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{2}}
in
R
.
We denote these two sets of special base vertices as
L^{*}_{s}
and
R^{*}_{s}
. We further define

L^{*}:=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in[k_{r}]\times[k_{r-1}]\times\ldots\times[k_{1}]\end{subarray}}L^{*}_{s}\qquad\text{and}\qquad R^{*}:=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in[k_{r}]\times[k_{r-1}]\times\ldots\times[k_{1}]\end{subarray}}R^{*}_{s}.

With this notation, we can now show that the special base vertices
L^{*}\cup R^{*}
correspond to the special base instances
(A^{*},B^{*})
using
Claim

5.20
.

Corollary 5.21
.

For
r\geqslant 1
, the subgraph of
G=\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
induced by the vertices
L^{*}\cup R^{*}
is exactly the vertex-disjoint union of the bit graphs
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{*}_{s},B^{*}_{s})
for all
s\in[k_{r}]\times[k_{r-1}]\times\ldots\times[k_{1}]
.

Proof.

We prove this by induction on
r
.

Base case for
\mathbf{r=1}
.

For every
s_{1}\in[k_{1}]
, we have the following directly from the above definitions:

L^{*}_{s}=L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{1}},R^{*}_{s}=R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{1}},A^{*}_{s}=A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{1}},B^{*}_{s}=B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{1}}.

Therefore,
L^{*}=L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
and
R^{*}=R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
. Then, by
Claim

5.20
, we have that the subgraph induced by the vertices
L^{*}
and
R^{*}
is a vertex-disjoint union of graphs

\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{1}},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{1}})=\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{*}_{s_{1}},B^{*}_{s_{1}})

for all
s_{1}\in[k_{1}]
.
Furthermore, for each
s_{1}\in[k_{1}]
,
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{*}_{s_{1}},B^{*}_{s_{1}})
is a bit graph since it corresponds to the special base instance
(A^{*}_{s_{1}},B^{*}_{s_{1}})
, which is an instance of
\mathsf{AHM}_{0}
.

Inductive step for
\mathbf{r\geqslant 2}
.

Let
H^{\textnormal{{base}}}:=G[L^{*}\cup R^{*}]
be the subgraph in the statement.
It will also be useful for us to consider the subgraph
H^{\textnormal{{spec}}}:=G[L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}\cup R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}]
.
Since
L^{*}\subseteq L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
and
R^{*}\subseteq R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
, we have that,

H^{\textnormal{{base}}}=G[L^{*}\cup R^{*}]=H^{\textnormal{{spec}}}[L^{*}\cup R^{*}].

Now, by
Claim

5.20
,
H^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
is a vertex-disjoint union of subgraphs

H^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r}}:=\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r}},B^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r}})
for
{s_{r}}\in[k_{r}]
.
Therefore, we only need to show that, for each
{s_{r}}\in[k_{r}]
, the subgraph
H^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r}}
, when restricted to the vertices in
L^{*}\cup R^{*}
is exactly a vertex-disjoint union of the bit graphs
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{*}_{s},B^{*}_{s})
for all
s\in\{{s_{r}}\}\times[k_{r-1}]\times\ldots\times[k_{1}]
.

Since the only vertices of
L^{*}\cup R^{*}
that are in
H^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r}}
are exactly its special base vertices, what we want immediately follows from the induction hypothesis applied to
H^{\textnormal{{spec}}}_{s_{r}}
for each
{s_{r}}\in[k_{r}]
.

We can also show that these graphs have perfect matchings.

Claim 5.22
.

For
r\geqslant 0
and any
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
, the graph
G=\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
contains a perfect matching, i.e., of size
2n_{r}
.

Proof.

We prove this claim by induction on
r
where the base case when
r=0
is trivial, i.e., we have that
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(0)},B^{(0)})
is a single bit graph, which has a perfect matching by
Definition

5.17
.

When
r\geqslant 1
, let
H=G[L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}\cup R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}]
be its subgraph induced on the special vertices. By
Claim

5.20
and the induction hypothesis, we immediately have that
H
contains a perfect matching. Hence, it remains to argue that there is a perfect matching in the subgraph

H^{\prime}=G[L\backslash L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}\cup R\backslash R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}].

By definition of the special vertices,
L\backslash L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
corresponds to
L[i]
where
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)\leqslant b_{r}\cdot\alpha
and, similarly,
R\backslash R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
corresponds to
R[j]
where
\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}\cdot\alpha
for
i,j\in[b_{r}]
.
Then, to show that there is a perfect matching in
H^{\prime}
, it is sufficient to argue that

G[L[i]\cup R[j]]\text{~for each~}i,j\in[b_{r}]\text{~where~}\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}}(i)=\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}(j)\leqslant b_{r}\cdot\alpha

has a perfect matching since these are vertex disjoint graphs that cover all of
L\backslash L^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
and
R\backslash R^{\textnormal{{spec}}}
.
We consider the case when
r
is odd and even separately.

Odd r.

By construction, Alice adds edges corresponding to
B^{(r-1)}_{i,j}
.
In order to obtain
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
, which has a perfect matching by the induction hypothesis, Bob would need to delete the edges corresponding to
A^{(r-1)}_{i,j}
since
r-1
is even.
Instead, Bob deletes no edges so
G[L[i]\cup R[j]]
is a superset of
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
, which implies that it also has a perfect matching.

Even r.

By construction, Alice deletes the edges corresponding to
B^{(r-1)}_{i,j}
.
In order to obtain
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
, which has a perfect matching by the induction hypothesis, Bob would need to add the edges corresponding to
A^{(r-1)}_{i,j}
since
r-1
is odd.
Instead, Bob adds a superset of these edges since he adds all possible edges

(\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,\bm{1}(n_{r-1}),L[i],R[j])\cup\textnormal{{edges}}(r-1,\bm{0}(n_{r-1}),L[i],R[j])).

Therefore,
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r-1)}_{i,j},B^{(r-1)}_{i,j})
is a subgraph of
G[L[i]\cup R[j]]
and has a perfect matching.

Finally, using these key structural properties, we show that any graph
G
has a perfect matching and that any
\beta
-approximate maximum matching in
G
includes many edges that correspond to the bit graphs of the special base instances.

Lemma 5.23
.

For
r\geqslant 1
, any
\beta
-approximate maximum matching
M
in
G=\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
identifies the bits of at least

n_{r}\cdot(1/\beta-2\alpha r)

many special base instances in
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
.

Proof.

By
Corollary

5.21
, we know that the edges in
H=G[L^{*}\cup R^{*}]
are able to identify the bits of the special base instance.
Therefore, we first obtain a bound on the number of edges in
M
that must have both endpoints in
L^{*}\cup R^{*}
, i.e., the number of its edges in
H
.

Observe that the number of edges in
M
that are not in
H
have at least one endpoint
not
in
L^{*}\cup R^{*}
, i.e., there are at most
|(L\cup R)\backslash(L^{*}\cup R^{*})|
many such edges.
Therefore,
M
has at least

|M|-|(L\cup R)\backslash(L^{*}\cup R^{*})|

edges in
H
.
Then, by definition of the special base vertices
L^{*}\cup R^{*}
and the choice of parameters in
Equation

20
and
Observation

5.4
, we have that

|L^{*}|=|R^{*}|=2\cdot k_{r}\cdot k_{r-1}\cdot\ldots\cdot k_{1}=2n_{r}\cdot(1-\alpha)^{r}

and

|(L\cup R)\backslash(L^{*}\cup R^{*})|=4n_{r}-4n_{r}\cdot(1-\alpha)^{r}=4n_{r}(1-(1-\alpha)^{r}).

Now, by
Claim

5.22
and since
M
is a
\beta
-approximation, we have that
M
is of size at least
2n_{r}/\beta
, which gives us the following bound:

\displaystyle|M|-|(L\cup R)\backslash(L^{*}\cup R^{*})|

\displaystyle\geqslant 2n_{r}/\beta-4n_{r}(1-(1-\alpha)^{r})

\displaystyle=4n_{r}(1/2\beta-1+(1-\alpha)^{r}).

Finally, by
Corollary

5.21
, the only edges in
H
are the edges of the vertex-disjoint union of the bit graphs
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{*}_{s},B^{*}_{s})
for
s\in[k_{r}]\times[k_{r-1}]\times\ldots\times[k_{1}]
.
By
Definition

5.17
, each bit graph is a matching of exactly two edges, each of which can be used to identify the underlying bit of the special base instance. Therefore, in the worst case, every two edges of
M
in
H
identifies a single bit of a special base instance and thus at least

\displaystyle 2n_{r}(1/2\beta-1+(1-\alpha)^{r})

\displaystyle\geqslant 2n_{r}(1/2\beta-1+1-\alpha\cdot r)

(by Bernoulli’s inequality)

\displaystyle=n_{r}\cdot(1/\beta-2\alpha\cdot r)

many such bits are identified by
M
.

5.2.3
A Protocol for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
using a Dynamic Streaming Algorithm

Using the above graph construction and its key properties, we can now construct a protocol
\pi
for any instance
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
that simulates a run of any
p
-pass
s
-space dynamic streaming algorithm
\mathcal{A}
for
\beta
-approximate maximum matching on the bipartite graph
G=\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
.
We then use this protocol to prove
Lemma

5.2
.

A protocol
\pi
for
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
on any input
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
using the
p
-pass
s
-space dynamic streaming algorithm
\mathcal{A}
where
r=2p-1
:

(a)

Alice computes the edge insertions
E_{A}=\textnormal{{edges}}(r,A^{(r)},L,R)
and then constructs an arbitrary ordering
\sigma_{A}
of the edges
E_{A}
.
Similarly, Bob computes the edge deletions
E_{B}=\textnormal{{edges}}(r,B^{(r)},L,R)
and then constructs an arbitrary ordering
\sigma_{B}
of the edges
E_{B}
.

(b)

The players simulate each pass of
\mathcal{A}
on the stream of insertions
\sigma_{A}
followed by deletions
\sigma_{B}
in the usual manner: To simulate a single pass, Alice runs
\mathcal{A}
on the insertions in
\sigma_{A}
then sends the memory state to Bob, who continues
\mathcal{A}
on the deletions in
\sigma_{B}
and sends the memory state back to Alice. In only the final pass, Bob computes the output matching
M
of
\mathcal{A}
instead of sending its memory state.

(c)

In parallel to the simulation, Alice and Bob also exchange messages to identify the labels of all the special base instances in
(A^{*},B^{*}
), each of which is identified by a tuple
(s_{r},s_{r-1},\ldots,s_{1})\in[k_{r}]\times[k_{r-1}]\times\ldots\times[k_{1}]
as follows.

•

In the first round, Alice does not send anything. In the second round, Bob sends the two input permutations
\sigma_{\textnormal{{r}}},\sigma_{\textnormal{{c}}}
of
[b_{r}]
along with the message to Alice, using which Alice can identify
k_{r}
many special sub-instances of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
.

•

For any round
2<t\leqslant r
, the player who receives the message of round
t-1
identifies the
k_{r}\cdot k_{r-1}\cdot\ldots\cdot k_{r-t+3}
many special instances of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-t+2}
(sub-instances of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-t+3}
). In round
t
, this player sends
2\cdot k_{r}\cdot k_{r-1}\cdot\ldots\cdot k_{r-t+3}
many permutations of
[b_{r-t+2}]
, corresponding to the two input permutations of each of these instances of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-t+2}
.

•

After receiving message of round
r
, Bob is able to identify the
k_{r}\cdot k_{r-1}\cdot\ldots\cdot k_{2}
many special instances of
\mathsf{AHM}_{1}
. Bob already knows the two input permutations of
[b_{1}]
associated with all these special instances of
\mathsf{AHM}_{1}
, and thus knows the labels of all the special base instances.

(d)

At the end of the protocol, the final player receives a uniform random search sequence
s^{*}=(k^{\star}_{r},k^{\star}_{r-1},\ldots,k^{\star}_{1})
. Then, using the information obtained in the previous step, the player identifies the special base instance
(A^{*}_{s^{*}},B^{*}_{s^{*}})
and corresponding special base vertices
L^{*}_{s^{*}}=\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}
and
R^{*}_{s^{*}}=\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
.
The player returns
x_{s^{*}}
as the output of the protocol, which is determined as follows:

•

If
M
contains the edge
(\ell_{1},r_{1})
or
(\ell_{2},r_{2})
,
x_{s^{*}}=0
;

•

If
M
contains the edge
(\ell_{1},r_{2})
or
(\ell_{2},r_{1})
,
x_{s^{*}}=1
;

•

Otherwise,
x_{s^{*}}
is a uniform random bit.

We now use this constructed protocol to prove the desired connection between maximum bipartite matching in the dynamic streaming model and the
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
problem,
thus proving
Lemma

5.2
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.2
.

To prove this lemma, we show that
\pi
is an
r
-round protocol with

\textsc{CC}(\pi)\leqslant r\cdot s+O(r^{2}\cdot n_{r}\log n_{r})\quad\text{and}\quad\textnormal{suc}(\pi)\geqslant 1/2+1/6\beta

where, as in the statement of the lemma,
r=2p-1
,
\alpha=1/(4\beta r)
, and
\beta\geqslant 1
is the approximation guarantee of the maximum matching returned by
\mathcal{A}
.

First, we argue the number of rounds of communication required by each step separately:

•

By
Claim

5.18
, Alice and Bob can compute their edges
E_{A}
and
E_{B}
without any communication.

•

In the simulation of
\mathcal{A}
, each pass of the algorithm is simulated using two rounds of communication, one message from Alice and one message from Bob, except for the final pass, which only requires one round, i.e., one message from Alice. Since
\mathcal{A}
has
p
passes, simulating it requires
r=2p-1
rounds of communication.

•

Identifying the labels of the special base instances (which is only required at the end to output the solution) also requires
r
rounds of communication since, at the end of each round
t\in[r]
,
k_{r}\cdot k_{r-1}\cdot\ldots\cdot k_{r-t+1}
many special instances of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r-t}
are identifiable by the player that receives the message.

•

Returning the output of protocol
\pi
is solely computed from the output of
\mathcal{A}
and the identified labels, which can be done without any further communication.

Since the simulation of
\mathcal{A}
and identifying the labels is done in parallel, protocol
\pi
is an
r
-round protocol.

Next, we argue the communication cost of
\pi
by considering separately the only two steps that require communication.
To simulate
\mathcal{A}
, the players only exchange the memory state of
\mathcal{A}
. Since this requires at most
s
bits for each of the
r
messages, the overall communication required in the simulation is at most
r\cdot s
bits.

Next, we give an upper bound on the communication required to communicate the labels. In round
t
for
2\leqslant t\leqslant r
, the player sends
2\cdot k_{r}\cdot k_{r-1}\cdot\ldots\cdot k_{r-t+3}
many permutations of
[b_{r-t+2}]
.
Using
Equation

20
, we can say, in total,

\displaystyle 2\cdot k_{r}\cdot k_{r-1}\cdot\ldots\cdot k_{r-t+3}\cdot b_{r-t+2}\cdot\log(b_{r-t+2})

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\cdot b_{r}\cdot b_{r-1}\cdot\ldots\cdot b_{r-t+3}\cdot b_{r-t+2}\cdot\log(b_{r-t+2})

\displaystyle=O(n_{r}\cdot\log(n_{r}))

bits are sent. In
r
rounds, the total number of bits to find the labels of all special instances is
O(r\cdot n_{r}\cdot\log(n_{r}))
.
Therefore,
\textsc{CC}(\pi)\leqslant r\cdot s+O(r\cdot n_{r}\log n_{r})
.

Finally, we argue the probability of success of
\pi
. The goal for solving the instance
(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
of
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}(n_{r},\alpha)
is to output the bit of the special base instance
(A^{*}_{s^{*}},B^{*}_{s^{*}})
where
s^{*}=(k^{\star}_{r},k^{\star}_{r-1},\ldots,k^{\star}_{1})
is the uniform random search sequence given to the final player, i.e., Bob since
r=2p-1
is odd, at the end of the protocol.
In protocol
\pi
, the predictor
x_{s^{*}}
of the solution is obtained from the matching
M
returned by
\mathcal{A}
or is a uniform random guess.

When
\mathcal{A}
succeeds, which occurs with
1-1/\mbox{\rm poly}{(n)}
probability, it is guaranteed to output a
\beta
-approximate maximum matching
M
in the bipartite graph
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
.
By
Lemma

5.23
, the edges of
M
identify the bits of at least
n_{r}\cdot(1/\beta-2\alpha r)
many special base instances, which are
n_{r}\cdot(1-\alpha)^{r}
many in total by
Observation

5.4
.
Since
s^{*}
uniformly selects one of the special base instances to correspond to the solution bit,
M
identifies it with probability at least

\displaystyle\frac{1/\beta-2\alpha r}{(1-\alpha)^{r}}\geqslant 1/\beta-2\alpha r=1/\beta-1/2\beta=1/2\beta

since
\alpha=1/(4\beta r)\in(0,1)
.

Thus, we have,

•

When
\mathcal{A}
succeeds and
M
correctly identifies the solution bit, the predictor
x_{s^{*}}
returned by
\pi
is correct (with probability
1
);

•

When
\mathcal{A}
succeeds but
M
does not identify the solution bit, the predictor
x_{s^{*}}
returned by
\pi
is a random guess and thus is correct with probability
1/2
;

•

When
\mathcal{A}
fails, there is no guarantee on the predictor bit, and we might as well assume the answer is wrong (with probability
1
);

As such, we have that the protocol succeeds with probability at least

\left(1-1/\mbox{\rm poly}{(n)}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2\beta}\cdot 1+\left(1-\frac{1}{2\beta}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{2}\right)+1/\mbox{\rm poly}{(n)}\cdot 0\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{3\beta}\right),

since
\beta
is a constant. This concludes the proof.

Remark 3
.

In the construction of
\textnormal{{graph}}(A^{(r)},B^{(r)})
, when
r
is even, Alice deletes the edges before Bob adds them: for the corresponding streaming problem, this corresponds to deleting edges that have not been inserted (although the final stream
still ensures that any edge that is deleted will be inserted); this is
not
consistent with the definition of dynamic graph streams.
Nevertheless, in our construction, we
only
use the
\mathsf{AHM}_{r}
lower bound when
r=2p-1
and thus is odd. Here, Alice adds edges to the graph, and Bob deletes the edges after all the edges are added. As a result, in the corresponding
streaming problem, no edge is deleted before it is inserted, thus adhering to the restriction of the dynamic graph streams.

Appendix

Appendix A
Background on Information Theory

We now briefly introduce some definitions and facts from information theory that are needed in this thesis. We refer the interested reader to the text by Cover and Thomas
[
CT06
]
for an excellent introduction to this field,
and the proofs of the statements used in this Appendix.

For a random variable
{\mathsf{A}}
, we use
\textnormal{\text{supp}}({\mathsf{A}})
to denote the support of
{\mathsf{A}}
and
\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}})
to denote its distribution.
When it is clear from the context, we may abuse the notation and use
{\mathsf{A}}
directly instead of
\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}})
, for example, write

A\sim{\mathsf{A}}
to mean
A\sim\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}})
, i.e.,
A
is sampled from the distribution of random variable
{\mathsf{A}}
.

•

We denote the
Shannon Entropy
of a random variable
{\mathsf{A}}
by

\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}})
, which is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}):=\sum_{A\in\textnormal{\text{supp}}({\mathsf{A}})}\Prob\left({\mathsf{A}}=A\right)\cdot\log{\left(1/\Prob\left({\mathsf{A}}=A\right)\right)}

(26)

•

The
conditional entropy
of
{\mathsf{A}}
conditioned on
{\mathsf{B}}
is denoted by
\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{B}})
and defined as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{B}}):=\Exp_{B\sim{\mathsf{B}}}\left[\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{B}}=B)\right],

(27)

where

\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{B}}=B)
is defined in a standard way by using the distribution of
{\mathsf{A}}
conditioned on the event
{\mathsf{B}}=B
in Eq (
26
).

•

The
mutual information
of two random variables
{\mathsf{A}}
and
{\mathsf{B}}
is denoted by

\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{B}})
and is defined:

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}):=\mathbb{H}(A)-\mathbb{H}(A\mid B)=\mathbb{H}(B)-\mathbb{H}(B\mid A).

(28)

•

The
conditional mutual information
is defined as
\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}\mid{\mathsf{C}}):=\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{C}})-\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{C}})
.

Useful Properties of Entropy and Mutual Information

We shall use the following basic properties of entropy and mutual information throughout.
Proofs of these properties mostly follow from convexity of the entropy function
and Jensen’s inequality and can be found in
[
CT06
, Chapter 2]
.

Fact A.1
.

Let
{\mathsf{A}}
,
{\mathsf{B}}
,
{\mathsf{C}}
, and
{\mathsf{D}}
be four (possibly correlated) random variables.

1.

0\leqslant\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}})\leqslant\log{\left|{\textnormal{\text{supp}}({\mathsf{A}})}\right|}
. The right equality holds
iff
\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}})
is uniform.

2.

\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}\mid{\mathsf{C}})\geqslant 0
. The equality holds iff
{\mathsf{A}}
and

{\mathsf{B}}
are
independent
conditioned on
{\mathsf{C}}
.

3.

Conditioning on a random variable reduces entropy
:

\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{C}})\leqslant\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{B}})
. The equality holds iff
{\mathsf{A}}\perp{\mathsf{C}}\mid{\mathsf{B}}
.

4.

Chain rule for mutual information
:
\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{B}}\,;{\mathsf{C}}\mid{\mathsf{D}})=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{C}}\mid{\mathsf{D}})+\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{B}}\,;{\mathsf{C}}\mid{\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{D}})
.

5.

Data processing inequality
: for a function
f({\mathsf{A}})
of
{\mathsf{A}}
,
\mathbb{I}(f({\mathsf{A}})\,;{\mathsf{B}}\mid{\mathsf{C}})\leqslant\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}\mid{\mathsf{C}})
.

We also use the following two standard propositions on effect of conditioning on mutual information.

Proposition A.2
.

For random variables
{\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{D}}
, if
{\mathsf{A}}\perp{\mathsf{D}}\mid{\mathsf{C}}
, then,

\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}\mid{\mathsf{C}})\leqslant\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}\mid{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{D}}).

Proof.

Since
{\mathsf{A}}
and
{\mathsf{D}}
are independent conditioned on
{\mathsf{C}}
, by

Fact

A.1
-(
3
),
\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{C}})=\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{D}})
and
\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{B}})\geqslant\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{D}})
. We have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}\mid{\mathsf{C}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{C}})-\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{B}})=\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{D}})-\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{B}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{D}})-\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{D}})=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}\mid{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{D}}).\penalty\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 15.00002pt\penalty\vrule height=7.5pt,width=5.0pt,depth=2.5pt

Proposition A.3
.

For random variables
{\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{D}}
, if
{\mathsf{A}}\perp{\mathsf{D}}\mid{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{C}}
, then,

\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}\mid{\mathsf{C}})\geqslant\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}\mid{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{D}}).

Proof.

Since
{\mathsf{A}}\perp{\mathsf{D}}\mid{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{C}}
, by
Fact

A.1
-(
3
),
\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{C}})=\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{D}})
. Moreover, since conditioning can only reduce the entropy (again by
Fact

A.1
-(
3
)),

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}\mid{\mathsf{C}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{C}})-\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{C}})\geqslant\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{D}},{\mathsf{C}})-\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{C}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{D}},{\mathsf{C}})-\mathbb{H}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{D}})=\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}\mid{\mathsf{C}},{\mathsf{D}}).\penalty\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 15.00002pt\penalty\vrule height=7.5pt,width=5.0pt,depth=2.5pt

Measures of Distance Between Distributions

We use two main measures of distance (or divergence) between distributions, namely the
Kullback-Leibler divergence
(KL-divergence) and the
total variation distance
.

KL-divergence.

For two distributions
\mu
and
\nu
over the same probability space, the
Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence
between
\mu
and
\nu
is denoted by
\mathbb{D}(\mu~||~\nu)
and defined as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\mu~||~\nu):=\Exp_{a\sim\mu}\Big[\log\frac{\mu(a)}{{\nu}(a)}\Big].

(29)

We also have the following relation between mutual information and KL-divergence.

Fact A.4
.

For random variables
{\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{C}}
,

\mathbb{I}({\mathsf{A}}\,;{\mathsf{B}}\mid{\mathsf{C}})=\Exp_{(B,C)\sim{({\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{C}})}}\Big[\mathbb{D}(\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{B}}=B,{\mathsf{C}}=C)~||~\textnormal{dist}({\mathsf{A}}\mid{\mathsf{C}}=C))\Big].

Total variation distance.

We denote the
total variation distance
between two distributions
\mu
and
\nu
on the same
support
\Omega
by
\|\mu-\nu\|_{\mathrm{tvd}}
, defined as:

\displaystyle\|\mu-\nu\|_{\mathrm{tvd}}:=\max_{\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega}\left(\mu(\Omega^{\prime})-\nu(\Omega^{\prime})\right)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{x\in\Omega}\left|{\mu(x)-\nu(x)}\right|.

(30)

We use the following basic properties of total variation distance.

Fact A.5
.

Suppose
\mu
and
\nu
are two distributions for
\mathcal{E}
, then,

{\mu}(\mathcal{E})\leqslant{\nu}(\mathcal{E})+\|\mu-\nu\|_{\mathrm{tvd}}.

We also have the following (chain-rule) bound on the total variation distance of joint variables.

Fact A.6
.

For any distributions
\mu
and
\nu
on
n
-tuples
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
,

\|\mu-\nu\|_{\mathrm{tvd}}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Exp_{X_{<i}\sim\mu}\|\mu(X_{i}\mid X_{<i})-\nu(X_{i}\mid X_{<i})\|_{\mathrm{tvd}}.

The following Pinsker’s inequality bounds the total variation distance between two distributions based on their KL-divergence,

Fact A.7

(Pinsker’s inequality)
.

For any distributions
\mu
and
\nu
,

\|\mu-\nu\|_{\mathrm{tvd}}\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbb{D}(\mu~||~\nu)}.
